will play an important role in Section
6
and Section
7
. Because inverting
{\operatorname{haze}}(z)
amounts to solving a cubic equation, Cardano’s Formula can be used to obtain a closed-form expression for
{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(h)
:

(4.24)

{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(3.3957x)=\frac{2\sqrt{x^{2}+3}}{3}\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}+\frac{1}{3}\tan^{-1}\!\left(\frac{-3\sqrt{-3x^{4}-33x^{2}+3}}{x^{3}+18x}\right)\right)-\frac{x}{3}.

Returning to the function
h(r)
, we define
{h_{\max}}=h(0.531)\approx 1.01967
. By Lemma
4.22
, this is slightly less than the true maximal value of
h
. Now, Theorem
4.21
and Lemma
4.22
have the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 4.25
.

The function
h
of Definition
4.20
has a well-defined inverse

h^{-1}\colon(0,{h_{\max}}]\to[0.531,\infty),

which can be computed via (
4.24
). Furthermore,
h^{-1}
is a decreasing function such that the maximal tube radius satisfies

R\geq h^{-1}(\mathcal{A}),

provided
R\geq 0.531
. ∎

4.2.
Injectivity radii

Recall the definition of
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{i})
from Definition
2.17
.

Theorem 4.26
.

Let
M
be a non-elementary hyperbolic cone-manifold, and
\Sigma^{+}
a geodesic link containing the singular locus. Let
U_{\max}(\Sigma^{+})
be the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma^{+}
, with smallest tube radius
R
.

Then, for every tube
U_{i}\subset U_{\max}
and every
x\in\partial U_{i}
,

(4.27)

\displaystyle 2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{i})

\displaystyle\geq 1.361\sqrt{1-\cos\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}\right)}\cdot\frac{\sinh R}{S(R)}

(4.28)

\displaystyle>1.1227\tanh R-0.1604.

where
S(R)
is defined in (
4.5
). The functions on the right-hand side of (
4.27
) and (
4.28
) are increasing in
R
.

Proof.

First, we check that the function expressing the lower bound in (
4.27
) is increasing in
R
, and calculate its limit as
R\to\infty
. We define

f_{1}(R)=\sqrt{1-\cos\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}\right)}\cdot\cosh(R),\qquad f_{2}(R)=\frac{1}{S(R)},\qquad f_{3}(R)=\tanh R,

so that the lower-bound function in (
4.27
) becomes
f(R)=1.361\,f_{1}(R)\,f_{2}(R)\,f_{3}(R)
.
The first non-constant term in the product can be written as

f_{1}(R)=\sqrt{1-\cos\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}\right)}\cdot\cosh(R)=\sqrt{\frac{1-\cos(A)}{A^{2}}}\qquad\text{for}\qquad A=\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}.

For
A
between
0
and
1
, the function
f_{1}(R)
is decreasing in
A
, hence increasing in
R
. It satisfies
\displaystyle{\lim_{R\to\infty}f_{1}(R)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}
.
The second term is
f_{2}(R)=S(R)^{-1}
, which is increasing by Claim
4.10
and approaches
1
as
R\to\infty
. Finally, the third term is
f_{3}(R)=\tanh R
, which is also increasing in
R
and approaches
1
as
R\to\infty
. Thus
f(R)=1.361\,f_{1}(R)\,f_{2}(R)\,f_{3}(R)
increases at least as fast as
Z=\tanh R
, and satisfies

(4.29)

\lim_{R\to\infty}f(R)=1.361\,\lim_{R\to\infty}f_{1}(R)\,f_{2}(R)\,f_{3}(R)=\frac{1.361}{\sqrt{2}}=0.96237\ldots

Next, we check that the function in (
4.27
) is larger than the one (
4.28
).
Set
Z=\tanh R
, as above.
When
Z\in[0.99995,\,1)
, the increasing function in (
4.27
) is bounded below by
0.9623
, whereas
1.1227Z-0.1604
is bounded above by
0.9623
. Meanwhile, when
Z\in[0,0.99995]
, we check using interval arithmetic in Sage that the function in (
4.27
) is larger than
1.1227Z-0.1604
. This is established by breaking the domain
[0,0.99995]
into small intervals and checking the desired inequality on each sub-interval. See the ancillary files
[
23
]
for details.

Now, we proceed to the main portion of the proof: the lower bound on
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{i})
expressed in (
4.27
). Consider the torus
T_{i}=\partial U_{i}
. Proposition
4.3
has the following consequence.

Claim 4.30
.

The torus
T_{i}=\partial U_{i}
contains an embedded open disk of radius

\frac{b(R,R)}{S(R)}=\frac{\sinh^{2}R}{S(R)\sinh(2R)}=\frac{\tanh R}{2S(R)}\,,

where
b(\cdot,\cdot)
is a semi-major axis as in (
4.4
), and
S(\cdot)
is as in (
4.5
).

This can be seen as follows.
By Proposition
4.3
, torus
T_{i}
contains an embedded ellipse, whose semi-major axes are

a(R_{i},R_{j})=\frac{\cosh R_{i}\sinh R_{j}}{S(R_{j})\cosh(R_{i}+R_{j})}\quad\mbox{and}\quad b(R_{i},R_{j})=\frac{\sinh R_{i}\sinh R_{j}}{\sinh(R_{i}+R_{j})}.

This ellipse contains a disk of radius
\min\{a,b\}
. We would like to determine this minimum. By Lemma
A.2
,

\frac{\cosh R_{i}}{\cosh(R_{i}+R_{j})}>\frac{\sinh R_{i}}{\sinh(R_{i}+R_{j})},\quad\mbox{hence}\quad S(R_{j})a(R_{i},R_{j})>b(R_{i},R_{j}).

Since
S(R_{j})>1
, it follows that

\min\left\{a(R_{i},R_{j}),\,b(R_{i},R_{j})\right\}\geq\min\left\{a(R_{i},R_{j}),\,\frac{b(R_{i},R_{j})}{S(R_{j})}\right\}=\frac{b(R_{i},R_{j})}{S(R_{j})}\geq\frac{b(R,R)}{S(R)}.

Here, the last inequality follows because
b(R_{i},R_{j})
is monotonically increasing in both variables, by
a calculation similar to
Lemma
4.15
. Meanwhile,
S(R)
is monotonically decreasing by Claim
4.10
, hence the quotient is increasing. This proves the claim.

Proceeding toward the main proof, let

\hat{M}
be the universal branched cover of
M
, branched over
\Sigma^{+}
.
Choose a preimage
\widetilde{U}_{i}
of
U_{i}
. Then
\widetilde{T}_{i}=\partial\widetilde{U}_{i}
is a Euclidean plane that covers
T_{i}
. Our goal, following Definition
2.17
, is to give a lower bound on the distance between a lift
\widetilde{x}
of
x
and any of its translates under
\pi_{1}(T_{i})={\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
.

By Claim
4.30
,
\widetilde{T_{i}}
contains a
{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
–equivariant family of disjoint disks, of radius
b(R,R)/S(R)
.
Fix
p=\widetilde{x}
, and let
q=\varphi(\widetilde{x})
be the closest translate of
p
. Since
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{i})
is constant over points of
T_{i}
, we may assume that
p
and
q
lie at centers of disks in this family. Thus
d_{E}(p,q)\geq 2b(R,R)/S(R)
, where
d_{E}
denotes the Euclidean distance on
\widetilde{T_{i}}
, as in Section
3.3
.

Claim 4.31
.

We have
d(p,q)\geq f(R)=\displaystyle{1.361\sqrt{1-\cos\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}\right)}\cdot\frac{\sinh R}{S(R)}}
.

Before proving this claim, we make some quick reductions. First, as we computed in (
4.29
), the function
f(R)
is bounded above by
0.9625
. Thus it suffices to assume
d(p,q)\leq 0.9625
.
Second, it suffices to assume that the disks of radius
b/S
centered at
p
and
q
are tangent, because any lower bound on distance for tangent disks will still apply as
p,q
are moved further apart.

Our lower bound on
d(p,q)
will come from Lemma
3.8
. In preparation for applying that lemma, we note that since
d(p,q)\leq 0.9625
, we have

(4.32)

\frac{\cosh d(p,q)-1}{d(p,q)^{2}}\leq\frac{\cosh 0.9625-1}{0.9625^{2}}=0.53981\ldots.

By Remark
4.14
, the
\theta
–coordinates of an ellipse centered at
(r,0,0)
must satisfy

(4.33)

|\theta|\leq\frac{1}{2\cosh(R_{j})}\leq\frac{1}{2\cosh(R)}=\frac{A}{2},

where recall that we defined
A=1/\cosh(R)
.
This means that the
\theta
–coordinates of
p
and
q
, whose disks are assumed to be tangent, must differ by at most
A
, which is at most
1<\pi
.
Finally, if the disks centered at
p,q
are tangent, Claim
4.30
implies

(4.34)

d_{E}(p,q)=\frac{2b(R,R)}{S(R)}=\frac{\tanh R}{S(R)}.

Now, we may plug (
4.32
) and (
4.34
) into the lower bound of Lemma
3.8
. Using the upper bound from (
4.32
), we obtain

0.53982\,d(p,q)^{2}\geq\cosh d(p,q)-1\geq\frac{1-\cos A}{A^{2}}d_{E}(p,q)^{2}=\frac{1-\cos A}{A^{2}}\cdot\frac{\tanh^{2}R}{S(R)^{2}}.

Using the value
A=1/\cosh R
from (
4.33
), this simplifies to

\displaystyle d(p,q)

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{0.53982}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{1-\cos A}}{A}\cdot\frac{\tanh R}{S(R)}

\displaystyle=1.36105\ldots\sqrt{1-\cos\left(\frac{1}{\cosh(R)}\right)}\cdot\cosh R\cdot\frac{\tanh R}{S(R)},

proving the claim. Since
q
was assumed to be the closest translate of
p=\widetilde{x}
, Claim
4.31

proves the theorem.
∎

Remark 4.35
.

If the cone manifold
M
has cusps, the constructions and results of this section also apply to a maximal neighborhood consisting of tubes and horocusps in
M
. To extend Definition
4.2
, first construct a maximal multi-tube as in that definition. Then, choose any ordering on the cusps and expand each cusp neighborhood until it bumps into a tube or a previously expanded cusp.

After such a construction, Theorems
4.16
and
4.26
hold for the boundary tori of both tubes and horocusps. One way to see this is to view horocusps as limiting cases of tubes with radius
R_{i}\to\infty
. A key point in the proofs of both Theorem
4.16
and Theorem
4.26
is that the relevant estimates are monotonically increasing in
R_{i}
. Thus they will also hold if
R_{i}
is replaced by
\infty
.

If there are no compact tubes at all, but only a union of maximal cusps, both theorems become well-known statements from the literature.
Theorem
4.16
becomes the well-known estimate due to Meyerhoff
[
44
, Section 5]
that every cusp torus
T_{i}=\partial U_{i}
satisfies

\operatorname{area}(T_{i})\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}=\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{3}\sinh^{2}R}{S(R)\cosh(2R)}.

Meanwhile, Theorem
4.26
becomes a well-known estimate observed by Adams
[
1
, Lemma 2.4]
: every non-trivial element
\varphi\in\pi_{1}(T_{i})
corresponds to a horocycle of length
\geq 1
. In other words, for every
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{T}_{i}
and every
1\neq\varphi\in\pi_{1}(T_{i})
, we have
d_{E}(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})\geq 1
. By Lemma
3.9
, this implies

2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{i})\>=\>\min\{d(\widetilde{x},\varphi(\widetilde{x})):\varphi\neq 1\}\>\geq\>2\arcsinh(1/2)\>=\>0.96242\ldots,

which is nearly the same as the asymptotic limit computed in (
4.29
).

5.
Existence of cone deformations

This section proves that if
M
is a hyperbolic manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
is a geodesic link that is sufficiently short, then there exists a cone deformation interpolating between
M
and
M-\Sigma
. See Theorem
5.1
for a precise statement.
This result is closely related to theorems of Hodgson and Kerckhoff
[
33
]
and Bromberg
[
14
]
showing that cone deformations exist under certain conditions. However, we need a version that has explicitly quantified hypotheses, allows for multiple components of
\Sigma
, and allows
M
to be a cusped manifold. Such a version did not previously appear in the literature. Still, our proof in this section relies heavily on the cone deformation theory developed by Hodgson and Kerckhoff
[
31
,
33
,
34
]
. In order to explain the statement and set up the proof, we review necessary background material from their work. Reviewing background from cone deformation theory will also allow us to define several important quantities and set up notation that will be used in the subsequent sections.

On the way to proving Theorem
5.1
, we will establish Theorem
5.14
, which provides quantitative control on the radius of a maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma
. This result will be used repeatedly in the sequel.

A related theorem of Hodgson and Kerckhoff
[
34
, Theorem 1.2]
provides an interpolation by cone manifolds from
M-\Sigma
to
M
(i.e. in the opposite direction of Theorem
5.1
), provided that all meridians on the cusps of
M-\Sigma
are sufficiently long. We recall their result below, in Theorem
5.17
, again adding quantitative control over the radius of a multi-tube about
\Sigma
.

In this section, and in the sequel,
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link. We use the notation

\ell_{j}=\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})
to denote the initial length of
\sigma_{j}
in a non-singular metric, and
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}(t)=\operatorname{len}_{t}(\sigma_{j})
to denote the length of
\sigma_{j}
in a changing metric
g_{t}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
M
be a finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Suppose that
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
, whose components have lengths satisfying

\ell_{j}=\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996\>\>\mbox{for all $j$}\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad\ell=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ell_{j}\leq 0.15601.

Then the hyperbolic structure on
M
can be deformed to a complete hyperbolic structure on
M-\Sigma
by decreasing the cone angle
\alpha_{j}
along
\sigma_{j}
from

2\pi
to
0
. The cone angles on all components of
\Sigma
change in unison.

Hodgson and Kerckhoff have shown this result in the setting where
M
is a closed hyperbolic 3–manifold and
\Sigma
is connected
[
33
, Corollary 6.3]
. In this special case, it suffices to assume that
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\leq 0.11058
.
Bromberg extended their result to geometrically finite manifolds without rank one cusps
[
14
, Theorem 1.2]
. However, his hypotheses are not explicitly quantified, while we need explicit bounds under explicit hypotheses.

5.1.
Background on cone deformations

Hodgson and Kerckhoff
[
31
]
show that an infinitesimal deformation of a cone manifold structure on
M
, with singular locus
\Sigma
, can be represented as a harmonic
1
–form
\omega
with values in the bundle
E
of infinitesimal isometries of
X=M-\Sigma
. Explicit information about
\omega
is used to determine the effect of the deformation on the singular locus.

Since
X
is a hyperbolic
3
–manifold, its bundle of infinitesimal isometries can be identified with
TX\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\cong TX\oplus i\,TX
. Here
(v,iw)\in TX\oplus i\,TX
corresponds to an infinitesimal translation in the direction of
v
and an infinitesimal rotation about an axis in the direction of
w
. In
[
34
]
, Hodgson and Kerckhoff show that
\omega
can be taken to be
harmonic
, which means it will have the form

(5.2)

\omega=\eta+i*D\eta

where
\eta
is a
TX
–valued
1
–form on
X
, and
*
is the Hodge star operator on forms on
X
that takes the vector-valued 2–form
D\eta
to a vector-valued 1–form. The forms
\eta
and
*D\eta
are both symmetric and traceless. Under an appropriate
L^{2}
integrability condition,
\omega
is the unique closed and co-closed harmonic form in its cohomology class; see Remark
5.18
for details.

Given any component
\sigma_{j}
of the singular locus
\Sigma
, Hodgson and Kerckhoff use cylindrical coordinates about
\sigma_{j}
to compute two explicit closed and co-closed forms. The first,
\omega_{m}=\eta_{m}+i*D\eta_{m}
, represents an infinitesimal deformation which decreases the cone angle but does not affect the real part of the complex length of the meridian. The second,
\omega_{\ell}=\eta_{\ell}+i*D\eta_{\ell}
, stretches the singular locus but leaves the holonomy of the meridian unchanged. The effects of
\omega_{m}
and
\omega_{\ell}
on the complex length of any peripheral curve were computed in
[
31
, pages 32–33]
and recorded in

[
33
, Lemma 2.1]
.

In the following lemma,
t
is a dummy variable expressing the “direction” of an infinitesimal change of metric. Part of the content of Theorem
5.6
will be that infinitesimal deformations can actually be promoted to local deformations, parametrized by
t
.

Lemma 5.3

(Lemma 2.1 of
[
33
]
)
.

The effects of
\omega_{m}
and
\omega_{\ell}
on the complex length
\mathcal{L}
of any peripheral curve are as follows.

(1)

If
\omega=\omega_{m}
, then
\frac{d}{dt}(\mathcal{L})=-2\mathcal{L}
.

(2)

If
\omega=\omega_{\ell}
, then
\frac{d}{dt}(\mathcal{L})=2\rm{Re}(\mathcal{L})
.

Any harmonic infinitesimal deformation affecting
\sigma_{j}
alone can be written in terms of these forms:

(5.4)

\displaystyle\omega=s_{j}\,\omega_{m}+(x_{j}+i\,y_{j})\,\omega_{\ell}+\omega_{c},

where
s_{j}
,
x_{j}
, and
y_{j}
are real constants, and
\omega_{c}
is an infinitesimal deformation that does not affect the holonomy of the meridian and longitude on the torus
T_{j}
of distance
R
from
\sigma_{j}
. We define
\omega_{0}
to be
\omega-\omega_{c}
.

Because only
\omega_{m}
affects the cone angle, the coefficient
s_{j}
determines the change in cone angle at
\sigma_{j}
for our given parametrization.

Lemma
5.3
implies that the effect of
\omega_{0}
on the complex length
\mathcal{L}_{j}
of
\sigma_{j}
is given by

(5.5)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(\mathcal{L}_{j})=-2s_{j}\mathcal{L}_{j}+2(x_{j}+iy_{j}){\rm{Re}}(\mathcal{L}_{j})

A central result of Hodgson and Kerckhoff
[
31
]
is that there always exists a local cone deformation that changes the cone angle on each component of
\Sigma
at the desired rate. In fact, we may let the deformation preserve some number of closed geodesics whose cone angle is not changing. The following is a special case of
[
31
, Theorem 4.8]
, with parametrization information added as in
[
34
, Page 1073]
.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
M
be a finite volume hyperbolic cone manifold with singular locus
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
, such that each component of
\Sigma
has cone angle
\alpha_{j}\leq 2\pi
. Let
\Sigma^{+}=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{m}
be a geodesic link containing
\Sigma
. Pick a vector
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
, where
s_{j}=0
for
j>n
. Then there is a local cone deformation
(M,\Sigma^{+},g_{t})
, parametrized by
t
, such that

(5.7)

\frac{d\alpha_{j}}{dt}=-2\alpha_{j}s_{j}\,.

Furthermore, the metric
g_{t}
is determined up to isometry by the vector
(\alpha_{1}(t),\ldots,\alpha_{n}(t),\alpha_{n+1},\ldots,\alpha_{m})
.

In our setting, we will consider deformations where each component of
\Sigma
has the same cone angle. As in
[
34
]
, we choose the parametrization
t=\alpha^{2}
for
0\leq\alpha\leq 2\pi
, and insist that
\alpha_{j}=\alpha
for every
j\leq n
.
When
\alpha>0
, equation (
5.7
) becomes

(5.8)

\displaystyle s_{j}=-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\frac{d\alpha}{dt}=\frac{-1}{4\alpha^{2}}\qquad\mbox{for all $j\leq n$}.

5.2.
Visual area and maximal tubes

Recall from Definition
4.18
that the visual area of the
j^{\rm th}
component of
\Sigma
is
\mathcal{A}_{j}=\alpha_{j}\lambda_{j}
, and the total visual area is
\mathcal{A}=\sum\mathcal{A}_{j}
. Recall as well the notion of a maximal multi-tube from Definition
4.2
. Our goal is to ensure that the radius of
U_{\max}
does not degenerate to
0
during the course of the cone deformation. We do this by showing that
\mathcal{A}
is monotonic in
\alpha
(Lemma
5.9
) and that small visual area implies deep tubes (Corollary
4.25
).

Lemma 5.9
.

Consider a local cone deformation
(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
.
Let
U_{\max}(\Sigma)
be the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma
, and let
R
be the smallest radius of the tubes in
U_{\max}
. Let
\operatorname{len}_{t}(\Sigma)=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}
denote the total length of
\Sigma
in the cone-metric
g_{t}
. If
Z=\tanh R\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
, and
t>0
, then

(5.10)

\frac{d}{dt}\operatorname{len}_{t}(\Sigma)\geq\frac{\operatorname{len}_{t}(\Sigma)}{2t}\cdot\frac{3Z^{2}-1}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}\geq 0.

Furthermore,

(5.11)

\frac{d\mathcal{A}}{dt}\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}}{2t}\left(\frac{3Z^{2}-1}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}+1\right)\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}}{2t}>0.

Proof.

In our setting, every component of
\Sigma
has the same angle
\alpha_{j}=\alpha
.
Define
v=\mathcal{A}/\alpha^{2}=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)/\alpha
. Then

\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)=\sum\lambda_{j}=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{\alpha}=\alpha\,v=\sqrt{t}\,v.

Consequently,

\frac{d\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)}{dt}=\frac{d(\sqrt{t}\,v)}{dt}=\sqrt{t}\,\frac{dv}{dt}+\frac{v}{2\sqrt{t}}=\frac{v}{2\sqrt{t}}\left(\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+1\right)=\frac{\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)}{2t}\left(\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+1\right).

By
[
34
, Proposition 5.5]
, the hypothesis
R\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}\geq-\frac{1}{\sinh^{2}R}\left(\frac{2\cosh^{2}R-1}{2\cosh^{2}R+1}\right)\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{d\alpha}{dt}=-\left(\frac{1-Z^{4}}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}\right)\frac{1}{2t},

where the last equality uses
Z=\tanh R
and
t=\alpha^{2}
. Then

(5.12)

\displaystyle\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+1\geq-\frac{1-Z^{4}}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}+1=\frac{3Z^{2}-1}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}.

Since
Z=\tanh R<1
, the denominator of the last expression is always positive. The numerator will be non-negative whenever
Z\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
, hence the whole expression in (
5.12
) is non-negative. Thus

\frac{d\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)}{dt}=\frac{\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)}{2t}\left(\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+1\right)\geq\frac{\operatorname{len}_{t}(\Sigma)}{2t}\cdot\frac{3Z^{2}-1}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}\geq 0,

establishing (
5.10
). For (
5.11
), we recall that
\mathcal{A}=\alpha^{2}v=tv
. Thus

\frac{d\mathcal{A}}{dt}=t\frac{dv}{dt}+v=\frac{v}{2}\left(\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+2\right)=\frac{\mathcal{A}}{2t}\left(\frac{2t}{v}\frac{dv}{dt}+2\right)\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}}{2t}\left(\frac{3Z^{2}-1}{Z^{2}(3-Z^{2})}+1\right).

Since the expression in (
5.12
) is non-negative, (
5.11
) follows.
∎

Our goal is to bound the tube radius throughout a cone deformation. Following Hodgson and Kerckhoff,
we do this by using Corollary
4.25
, which can be rephrased as follows:
if the tube radius at some initial time
t
is larger than
0.531
, and
\mathcal{A}(t)
remains smaller than
{h_{\max}}\approx 1.0196
throughout the cone deformation, then the tube radius will remain large.

To apply Lemma
5.9
, we need to ensure that the cone-locus
\Sigma
has a tube of radius
R\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})>0.531
. This minimal assumption on tube radius will appear in many results below.

5.3.
Decreasing cone angles to
0

Recall that by Theorem
5.6
, there always exists a local cone deformation on
(M,\Sigma)
that decreases the cone angle on each component of
\Sigma
from
\alpha
to
\alpha-\epsilon
, for some small
\epsilon>0
.
To show that the cone deformation can be continued, we apply a result of Hodgson and Kerckhoff.

Theorem 5.13

(Theorem 3.12 of
[
33
]
)
.

Suppose
M_{t}
for
t\in[0,t_{\infty})
is a smooth path of finite volume hyperbolic cone manifold structures on
(M,\Sigma)
with cone angle
\alpha_{j}(t)
along the
j^{\rm th}
component of the singular locus
\Sigma
. Suppose
\alpha_{j}(t)\in[0,2\pi]
for all
t
, and
\alpha_{j}(t)\to a_{j}
as
t\to t_{\infty}
. Suppose there is a constant
{R_{\min}}>0
such that there is an embedded tube of radius at least
{R_{\min}}
around
\Sigma
for all
t
. Then the path extends continuously to
t=t_{\infty}
so that as
t\to t_{\infty}
,
M_{t}
converges in the bilipschitz topology to a cone manifold structure
M_{\infty}
on
M
with cone angle
a_{j}
along the
j^{\rm th}
component of
\Sigma
.

Proof.

This theorem is exactly
[
33
, Theorem 3.12]
, except for three minor differences in the statement:

(1)

The result
[
33
, Theorem 3.12]
is stated for closed manifolds rather than finite-volume manifolds.

(2)

It is stated for cone structures where all cone angles around the singular locus agree. In fact, we can be more flexible with parametrizing the deformation.

(3)

It is stated for cone-manifolds satisfying a uniform upper volume bound, independent of
t
.

Hypothesis (
3
) can be omitted because it holds automatically. This follows from a construction of Agol
[
3
]
, as follows. Agol uses the cone-metric
g_{t}
(which is non-singular outside a tube about
\Sigma
) to construct a complete metric of pinched negative curvature on
M-\Sigma
, which we denote
h_{t}
. The sectional curvatures of this metric are bounded in terms of the constant
{R_{\min}}>0
, while
\operatorname{vol}(h_{t})
differs from
\operatorname{vol}(g_{t})
by a multiplicative factor that depends only on
{R_{\min}}
. Furthermore, by a result of Boland, Connell, and Souto
[
7
]
,
\operatorname{vol}(h_{t})
differs by a bounded multiplicative factor from the volume of the complete hyperbolic metric, denoted
\operatorname{vol}(M-\Sigma)
. Consequently, Agol’s work gives a uniform upper bound on
\operatorname{vol}(M_{t})
as a function of
{R_{\min}}
and
\operatorname{vol}(M-\Sigma)
.
1
1

1

The main result of Agol’s paper
[
3
, Theorem 2.1]
uses this construction to bound the ratio
\operatorname{vol}(M-\Sigma)/\operatorname{vol}(M_{t})
from above. However the same ingredients also suffice to bound
\operatorname{vol}(M-\Sigma)/\operatorname{vol}(M_{t})
from below.

Hypothesis (
2
) can be omitted because it is never used in the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 3.12]
. The proof goes through verbatim without this assumption.

Issue (
1
) can now be resolved by an appeal to (
2
). Let
\Sigma^{+}
consist of geodesics and cusps, where the cusps have cone angle
0
. Now apply
[
33
, Theorem 3.12]
to
\Sigma^{+}
, so that the cone angle remains
0
on all cusps that remain unfilled. This immediately gives the result for finite volume manifolds.
∎

Theorem 5.14
.

Suppose
M
is a finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold, and
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
, of total length
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\leq 0.15601\approx h(\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3}))/(2\pi)
. Let
R
be the radius of a maximal embedded tube about
\Sigma
, and assume
R\geq 0.531
.
Define
{R_{\min}}=h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)
, and note that this value exists by Corollary
4.25
.

Then the hyperbolic structure on
M
can be deformed to the complete hyperbolic structure on
M-\Sigma
by decreasing the cone angles on
\Sigma
from
2\pi
to
0
in such a way that at any time
t
,

(1)

Every component of
\Sigma
has cone angle
\alpha=\sqrt{t}
,

(2)

The tube radius in
M_{t}
about
\Sigma
is
R(t)\geq{R_{\min}}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
,

(3)

If
t>0
, we have
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(t)>0
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.6
, there exists a cone deformation with cone angles near
2\pi
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
. At the maximal value of
t
, namely
t=(2\pi)^{2}
, we have

\mathcal{A}(t)=2\pi\ell=h({R_{\min}})\leq h(\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3}))<{h_{\max}},

hence
R\geq{R_{\min}}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
by Corollary
4.25
. By Lemma
5.9
, we have
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}((2\pi)^{2})>0
.

Let
I\subset[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
be the maximal subinterval containing
(2\pi)^{2}
, such that conclusions (
1
), (
2
), and (
3
) all hold for
t\in I
. In the previous paragraph, we checked that

I
contains
(2\pi)^{2}
, so is not empty.

Next, we show that
I
is open. Suppose
t_{0}\in I
, so there exists a hyperbolic cone manifold structure on
M
with cone angles
\alpha_{0}=\sqrt{t_{0}}
. On a small neighborhood of
t_{0}
, condition (
1
) holds as a consequence of Theorem
5.6
: there exists a local cone deformation with cone angles near
\alpha_{0}
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
for
t
near
t_{0}
.
Condition (
3
) is an open condition, hence
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(t)>0
in a small neighborhood of
t_{0}
in
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
. Therefore, in the union of
I
and this small neighborhood,
we have
\mathcal{A}(t)\leq\mathcal{A}((2\pi)^{2})\leq h({R_{\min}})
, hence
R\geq{R_{\min}}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
by Corollary
4.25
. So condition (
2
) is satisfied as well in this neighborhood, and
I
is open.

Now, we show that
I
is closed. Let
t_{\infty}=\inf I
. By Theorem
5.13
, the assumption that
R(t)\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
for
t\in I
implies that the cone deformation extends to time
t_{\infty}
, hence (
1
) holds. Second, note that (
2
) is a closed condition, hence
R(t_{\infty})\geq{R_{\min}}
by continuity. Third, by Lemma
5.9
,
R(t_{\infty})\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
implies that if
t_{\infty}>0
, then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(t_{\infty})>0
, hence condition (
3
) holds. Finally, if
t_{\infty}=0
, then condition (
3
) holds vacuously. Therefore,
I
is closed.

Since
I
is open, closed, and non-empty, it follows that
I=[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, hence the desired cone deformation interpolates all the way between cone angle
2\pi
and
0
.
∎

The style of argument in the above proof will be employed several more times in the paper. Conditions (
1
)–(
3
) are mutually reinforcing, with the property that if they hold on an interval
I
, then they also hold on a slightly larger interval. If
I
is closed, the conclusions hold on a neighborhood of the endpoint; if
I
is open, they hold on the closure. This continuous analogue of induction will be called a
crawling argument
.

We will prove Theorem
5.1
by applying Theorem
5.14
. Theorem
5.14
needs a hypothesis on the length
\ell

and a hypothesis on the radius of the maximal tube. Meanwhile, Theorem
5.1
only has hypotheses on length.
It turns out that for non-singular manifolds, the tube radius can be estimated from length alone.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
M
be a hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a geodesic link with components
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
, such that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
.
Then the maximal embedded tube about
\Sigma
has radius
R>0.531
.

See
[
28
, Proposition 1.10]
for a very similar statement, with slightly different numbers in the hypotheses and the conclusion. Our proof, using results of Meyerhoff
[
44
]
, is based on the proof of that proposition.

Proof of Lemma
5.15
.

This follows from a theorem of Meyerhoff
[
44
, Section 3]
.
For each
j
, let
\mathcal{L}_{j}=\ell_{j}+i\tau_{j}
be the complex length of
\sigma_{j}
. For each
j
, Meyerhoff constructs an embedded tube about
\sigma_{j}
whose radius
r_{j}
satisfies

\sinh^{2}r_{j}=\frac{\sqrt{1-2k(\mathcal{L}_{j})}}{2k(\mathcal{L}_{j})}-\frac{1}{2},\quad\text{where}\quad k(\mathcal{L}_{j})=\min_{m\in{\mathbb{N}}}\{\cosh(m\ell_{j})-\cos(m\tau_{j})\}.

Furthermore, the tubes about different components are disjoint
[
44
, Section 7]
.

Observe that
\frac{\sqrt{1-2k}}{2k}
is a decreasing function of
k
when
k\in(0,\sqrt{2}-1)
, and that
r_{j}=0.531
when
k(\mathcal{L}_{j})=0.34932\ldots
. Thus it remains to show that
k(\mathcal{L}_{j})\leq 0.34932
for all
\ell_{j}\in[0,0.0996]
and
\tau_{j}\in[0,2\pi]
. Since
\cosh(m\ell_{j})
is an increasing function of
\ell_{j}
, it suffices to set
\ell_{j}=0.0996
. Since
\cos(m\tau_{j})
is an even function of
\tau_{j}
, it suffices to consider values
\tau_{j}\in[0,\pi]
.

Finally, we claim that for every
\tau_{j}\in[0,\pi]
, there is an integer
m\in\{1,\ldots,8\}
such that
\cosh(m\cdot 0.0996)-\cos(m\tau_{j})\leq 0.34932
. This is verified using interval arithmetic in Sage; see the ancillary files
[
23
]
for details.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Suppose that
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
, such that each component has length
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
and
\sum\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.15601
. Since
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for each
j
, Lemma
5.15
says the maximal tubular neighborhood of
\Sigma
has radius
R>0.531
. Since
\ell=\sum\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.15601
, Theorem
5.14
implies that we may deform the cone angles on
\sigma_{j}
downward from
2\pi
to
0
.
∎

5.4.
Increasing cone angles from
0

Next, we present a companion result to Theorem
5.14
, whose hypotheses are on the drilled manifold
M-\Sigma
rather than the on the filled manifold
M
where
\Sigma
is non-singular. Recall that normalized length was defined in Definition
1.3
. If the total normalized length of all meridians in
M-\Sigma
is sufficiently large, one obtains a cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, with control on tube radii.

Definition 5.16
.

Define a function
I\colon(0,1)\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

I(z)=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{3.3957\,(1-z)}\,\exp\left(\int_{z}^{1}\frac{1+4w+6w^{2}+w^{4}}{(1+w)(1+w^{2})^{2}}dw\right),

where
z=\tanh r
as usual. This function has a unique critical point: a global minimum when
z=\sqrt{\sqrt{5}-2}
, with minimum value
56.469\ldots
.
The function is monotonically increasing for larger
z
, hence for
r\geq 0.531
. It blows up as
z\to 1
. See
[
33
, Pages 409–410]
.

Hodgson and Kerckhoff proved the following result.

Theorem 5.17
.

Let
M
be a
3
–manifold with empty or toroidal boundary, and
\Sigma
a smoothly embedded link in
M
. Suppose that
M-\Sigma
is a cusped hyperbolic manifold such that the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies

L^{2}\geq I({Z_{\min}}),\quad\mbox{where}\quad{Z_{\min}}=\tanh({R_{\min}})\geq 1/\sqrt{3}.

Then
M
admits a hyperbolic metric in which
\Sigma
is isotopic to a union of geodesics. Furthermore, the hyperbolic structure on
M-\Sigma
can be deformed to that of
M
via a family of cone-manifolds
M_{t}
, while maintaining the following properties.

(1)

Every component of
\Sigma
has the same cone angle in
M_{t}
,

(2)

The tube radius in
M_{t}
about
\Sigma
is
R(t)\geq{R_{\min}}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
,

(3)

\mathcal{A}(t)<{h_{\max}}
.

Proof.

This is essentially
[
34
, Theorem 5.11]
, with information about tube radius extracted from the proof.
By the remark following
[
31
, Theorem 4.8]
, there is a family of cone-manifolds
(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
for
t\in[0,\epsilon)
, in which the cone angles on
\Sigma
agree for each
t
.
In
[
34
, Theorem 5.8]
, Hodgson and Kerckhoff prove that the deformation can be continued so long as
R(t)\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
. Meanwhile, in

[
34
, Theorem 5.7]
and the discussion preceding the theorem, they
show that so long as
L^{2}\geq I({Z_{\min}})
, and the cone angles are at most
2\pi
, the tube radius
R(t)
will stay bounded below by
{R_{\min}}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
. Thus the deformation can be continued all the way up to cone angle
2\pi
, where we reach the complete hyperbolic metric on
M
. The link
\Sigma
is geodesic in each cone metric
g_{t}
, hence is also geodesic in the non-singular metric at cone angle
2\pi
.
∎

We conclude this section with a particularly natural choice of the harmonic form
\omega
.

Remark 5.18
.

Recall from Section
5.1
that an infinitesimal deformation of the cone-metric
g_{t}
is determined by a harmonic
1
–form
\omega
defined on
X=M-\Sigma
, with values in the bundle
E\cong TX\oplus iTX
of infinitesimal isometries of
X
. By Theorem
5.6
, the local family of cone-metrics
g_{t}
is determined up to isometry by its cone-angles, but different choices of
\omega
within the same cohomology class in
H^{1}(X,E)
lead to different choices of cone-metric within the same isometry class. In our bilipschitz theorem in Section
8
, it will be important to have a natural way to identify points of
(M,\Sigma,g_{a})
with points of
(M,\Sigma,g_{b})
, for the purpose of comparing the metrics
g_{a}
and
g_{b}
at a point
p\in X
. To that end, we pin down a canonical choice of
\omega
.

Suppose
\widetilde{\omega}
is a smooth
E
–valued
1
–form on
X=M-\Sigma
. In
[
31
, Theorem 2.7]
, Hodgson and Kerckhoff prove that so long as all cone angles are at most
2\pi
, which is always the case in our setting, there is a unique closed and co-closed harmonic form
\omega
such that
[\widetilde{\omega}]=[\omega]\in H^{1}(X,E)
, and furthermore
\widetilde{\omega}-\omega=ds
, where
s
is a globally defined
L^{2}
section of
E
. This choice of
\omega
determines the one-parameter family of cone-metrics
g_{t}
on the nose, and defines a
natural identity map

{\mathop{id}}\colon(M-\Sigma,g_{a})\to(M-\Sigma,g_{b})
that allows us to compare the metrics at any given point. Because of the canonical way in which
\omega
is chosen, the identity map conjugates every isometry of
(M,\Sigma,g_{a})
to an isometry of
(M,\Sigma,g_{b})
. We will say, for short, that the identity map is
equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of
(M,\Sigma)
.

In Sections
8
and
9
, we will always use this
1
–form
\omega
and the accompanying identity map. In Section
7
, where we will need the flexibility to enlarge
\Sigma
to a larger link
\Sigma^{+}
containing a non-singular geodesic, we will accordingly choose a harmonic form
\omega
with reference to
\Sigma^{+}
.

6.
Bounding the boundary terms

In this section, we will find explicit bounds on certain boundary terms that arise in the cone deformation. These boundary terms were used in
[
31
]
to prove that there are no infinitesimal deformations of hyperbolic cone manifolds fixing the cone angles. They have been used in many other applications of cone deformations to obtain geometric control. We will use boundary terms in Section
7
to bound the change in length of a non-singular geodesic, and in Section
8
to get bilipschitz estimates in the thick part of a manifold.

This section is quite technical, reviewing definitions and results from
[
31
,
33
,
34
]
that require significant work from analysis and differential geometry to state and to prove. For our applications, we need only the results (technical though they are), and not the analysis. Therefore we will skim over some of the definitions and results quickly, sweeping the complicated work of
[
31
,
33
,
34
]
into the references, pointing the reader to statements in those papers for careful definitions and details. Our goal in being brief is to attempt to avoid unnecessary complications that are peripheral to our applications. The reader interested only in the applications can view this section as a black-box, while the reader with more interest in cone deformations can still turn to the references for details.

6.1.
Definitions and setup

Throughout,
(M,\Sigma)
will be a hyperbolic cone manifold. We will also consider a submanifold
X\subset M
with
tubular boundary
: This means that
X
is either a model tube, or the complement of some number of model tubes.
We orient the boundary of
X
by
inward
normal vectors. This orientation will be important, as it affects the signs of our results.

Recall from Section
5.1
that an infinitesimal deformation of a cone manifold structure can be represented by a harmonic
1
–form
\omega
, and that we made a canonical choice of
\omega
in Remark
5.18
. The harmonic form
\omega
decomposes as
\omega=\eta+i*D\eta
, as in (
5.2
). In
[
31
, Proposition 1.3 and page 36]
, Hodgson and Kerckhoff show that integrating by parts over the submanifold
X
, again oriented by inward normal, gives

(6.1)

\int_{X}\|\omega\|^{2}dV=\int_{X}||D\eta||^{2}+||\eta||^{2}\,dV=\int_{\partial X}*D\eta\wedge\eta.

See also
[
33
, Lemma 2.3]
for a formulation of the result in notation that better matches ours.

The term on the far right of (
6.1
) is important. Thus Hodgson and Kerckhoff define the
boundary term

b_{X}
on
TX
–valued 1–forms
\mu
and
\nu
as follows.

(6.2)

b_{X}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\partial X}*D\mu\wedge\nu.

Thus the term on the far right of (
6.1
) becomes
b_{X}(\eta,\eta)
.

Next, recall from (
5.4
) and the ensuing discussion that
\omega
can be written as a sum
\omega=\omega_{0}+\omega_{c}
where
\omega_{0}
is written in terms of the explicit forms
\omega_{m}
and
\omega_{\ell}
that affect meridian and longitude, and
\omega_{c}
is a correction term. We may write
\omega_{0}=\eta_{0}+i*D\eta_{0}
and
\omega_{c}=\eta_{c}+i*D\eta_{c}
. Then (
6.1
) becomes

(6.3)

\int_{X}\|\omega\|^{2}dV=b_{X}(\eta,\eta)=b_{X}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})+b_{X}(\eta_{c},\eta_{c}),

using
[
33
, Lemma 2.5]
(the cross terms vanish).
See
[
34
, Equation (6) and (7)]
, where integration is implicit in their definition of the
L^{2}
norm.

We emphasize that the above formulas (
6.1
)–(
6.3
) hold both when
X
is a model tube and when
X
is the complement of some number of model tubes. This flexibility will be important in Section
7
.

For the rest of this section, and in Section
8
, boundary terms will appear in the following specific context.
Let
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})
be a vector of positive radii. Suppose that
U_{\mathbf{r}}=U_{\mathbf{r}}(\Sigma)
is an embedded multi-tube about the singular locus
\Sigma
, as in Definition
4.1
, and let
X_{\mathbf{r}}=M-U_{\mathbf{r}}
. The inward normal vectors that orient
\partial X_{\mathbf{r}}
point away from
\Sigma
.
For any
TX_{\mathbf{r}}
–valued
1
–forms
\mu
and
\nu
, define

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\mu,\nu)=b_{X_{\mathbf{r}}}(\mu,\nu)=\int_{\partial X_{\mathbf{r}}}*D\mu\wedge\nu.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n})
, where
\tanh(r_{j})\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
for all
j
. Then

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{c},\eta_{c})\leq 0.

Proof.

Let
U
be a solid torus of radius
r
. Then the principal curvatures of
\partial U
are
k_{1}=\tanh r
and
k_{2}=\coth r
. See, for instance,
[
34
, Page 1066]
. Thus, under the hypotheses of the lemma, the principal curvatures along every component
\partial X
satisfy
1/\sqrt{3}\leq k_{1}\leq k_{2}\leq\sqrt{3}
.

Under this hypothesis on principal curvatures, Hodgson and Kerckhoff prove in
[
34
, Theorem 4.2]
that
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{c},\eta_{c})\leq 0
.
∎

We remark that the hypotheses of Lemma
6.4
also imply
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})>0
. See
[
34
, Corollary 4.3]
.

We will need an upper bound on
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta,\eta)
.
By Lemma
6.4
, this amounts to finding an upper bound on
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})
.

Lemma 6.5
.

With the parametrization
t=\alpha^{2}
, the boundary term
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})
satisfies

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{4(1-z_{j}^{2})}{z_{j}^{2}(3-z_{j}^{2})}\cdot\frac{1}{16\alpha^{4}}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{j}

where
z_{j}=\tanh(r_{j})
, and
\alpha
is the cone angle, and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
is the visual area.

Proof.

This result is contained in the proof of
[
34
, Proposition 5.4]
. On the bottom of page 1074 and the top of page 1075, it is shown that

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{4a_{j}c_{j}-b_{j}^{2}}{4a_{j}}s^{2}\mathcal{A}_{j}

where
s=\frac{-1}{2\alpha}\frac{d\alpha}{dt}
, and where

a_{j}
,
b_{j}
, and
c_{j}
are as in
[
34
, equation (32)]
:

\displaystyle a_{j}=\frac{-\sinh^{2}r_{j}}{\cosh^{2}r_{j}}(2\cosh^{2}r_{j}+1),\qquad b_{j}=\frac{-2}{\cosh^{2}r_{j}},\qquad c_{j}=\frac{2\cosh^{2}r_{j}-1}{\sinh^{2}r_{j}\cosh^{2}r_{j}}.

As in
[
34
, page 1079]
, we let
t=\alpha^{2}
. Thus, as in (
5.8
), we have
s=-1/(4\alpha^{2})
.
Rewriting
a_{j}
,
b_{j}
, and
c_{j}
in terms of
z_{j}
, using Lemma
A.1
, gives the result.
∎

6.2.
Controlling length and visual area

The next several lemmas prove estimates relating how visual area changes under cone deformations. These results culminate in an estimate relating the normalized length
L
, measured on cusps in the complete metric on
M-\Sigma
, to the length
\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)
in the complete metric on
M
. This will feed into the bound on boundary terms later in this section.

Remark 6.6
.

We recall notation that will be used below. As usual, we are assuming that a cone deformation
M_{t}
is parameterized by
t=\alpha^{2}
where
\alpha
is the cone angle along each component of the singular locus
\Sigma
. We let
R
denote the smallest radius in a maximal multi-tube
U
about
\Sigma
in
M_{t}
.
If
\sigma_{j}
is a component of the singular locus, with length
\ell_{j}
, recall from Definition
4.18
that the visual area of the tube component
U_{j}
of
U
about
\sigma_{j}
is defined to be
\mathcal{A}_{j}=\alpha\ell_{j}
. The visual area of the union of all tubes is
\mathcal{A}=\sum\mathcal{A}_{j}
.

In the proof of Lemma
5.9
, we introduced the
variable
v=\mathcal{A}/\alpha^{2}
. We now let
u=1/v
. As above, we set
Z=\tanh(R)
.

Lemma 6.7
.

Suppose that
Z=\tanh R\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
. Let
u(t)=u(\alpha^{2})=\alpha^{2}/\mathcal{A}
. Then
du/dt
satisfies

-G(Z)\leq\frac{du}{dt}\leq\widetilde{G}(Z)

where

G(z)=\frac{1+z^{2}}{6.7914\,z^{3}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\widetilde{G}(z)=\frac{(1+z^{2})^{2}}{6.7914\,z^{3}\,(3-z^{2})}.

Furthermore,
G(z)
and
\widetilde{G}(Z)
are strictly decreasing on the interval
(0,1)
.

Proof.

The bound on
du/dt
is proved on page 1079 of
[
34
]
. The behavior of
G(z)
and
\widetilde{G}(z)
can be checked by differentiation.
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose we have a cone deformation from cone angle
0
to
\alpha>0
,
so that throughout the deformation, the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma
has radius
R\geq{R_{\min}}
, where
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
.
Then the function

u(\alpha^{2})=u(t)=\alpha^{2}/\mathcal{A}
satisfies:

L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})\alpha^{2}<u(\alpha^{2})<L^{2}+\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\alpha^{2}.

Here
L
is the total normalized length of the meridians of the drilled manifold
M-\Sigma
, as in Definition
1.3
.
In particular, for
0<\alpha\leq 2\pi
,

L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}<u(\alpha^{2})<L^{2}+\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}.

Proof.

Hodgson and Kerckhoff showed that as cone angle decreases to
0
, we have

u(0)=\lim_{t\to 0}\,u(t)=L^{2}.

See
[
34
, page 1076]
. Then at time
\alpha^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=u(0)+\int_{0}^{\alpha^{2}}\frac{du}{d\tau}d\tau

\displaystyle\geq L^{2}-\int_{0}^{\alpha^{2}}G(Z(\tau))\,d\tau\quad\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{Lem:hkp1079}}

\displaystyle>L^{2}-\int_{0}^{\alpha^{2}}G({Z_{\min}})\,d\tau\quad\mbox{using strict monotonicity of $G$}

\displaystyle=L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})\,\alpha^{2}.

The upper bound is obtained similarly, using the strict monotonicity of
\widetilde{G}
.
∎

A very similar argument gives the following.

Lemma 6.9
.

Suppose we have a cone deformation from cone angle
2\pi
to
\alpha
, so that throughout the cone deformation, the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma
has radius
R\geq{R_{\min}}
, where
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
.
Then for
\alpha<2\pi
, the function
u(\alpha^{2})=u(t)=\alpha^{2}/\mathcal{A}
satisfies:

\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})((2\pi)^{2}-\alpha^{2})<u(\alpha^{2})<\frac{2\pi}{\ell}+G({Z_{\min}})((2\pi)^{2}-\alpha^{2})

where
\ell
denotes the total length of
\Sigma
at cone angle
2\pi
.
In particular,

\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}<u(\alpha^{2})<\frac{2\pi}{\ell}+G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}.

Proof.

At cone angle
2\pi
, we have
t=(2\pi)^{2}
, hence

u((2\pi)^{2})=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\mathcal{A}}=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\sum\alpha\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})}=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}.

Now, we can set up an integral, as above:

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=u((2\pi)^{2})-\int_{\alpha^{2}}^{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{du}{d\tau}d\tau

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\int_{\alpha^{2}}^{(2\pi)^{2}}\widetilde{G}(Z(\tau))\,d\tau\quad\mbox{by Lemma~\ref{Lem:hkp1079}}

\displaystyle>\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\int_{\alpha^{2}}^{(2\pi)^{2}}\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\,d\tau\quad\mbox{using strict monotonicity of $\widetilde{G}$}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\,((2\pi)^{2}-\alpha^{2}).

The upper bound is obtained similarly, using the monotonicity of
G
.
∎

We can now relate the total normalized length
L
to the total length
\ell=\ell(\Sigma)
at cone angle
2\pi
. This result generalizes a lemma of Magid
[
43
, Lemma 4.7]
to cone deformations with multiple components, while sharpening the estimate and making hypotheses explicit.
It also converts the asymptotic formula of Neumann and Zagier
[
48
, Proposition 4.3]
into a two-sided inequality.

Lemma 6.10
.

Suppose that
M
is a complete finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Fix a constant
{R_{\min}}>0
such that
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}\geq 0.6622
; note this is strictly larger than
1/\sqrt{3}
. Suppose that
\Sigma\subset M
is a geodesic link such that one of the following hypotheses holds.

(1)

In the complete structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies
L^{2}\geq I({Z_{\min}})
, where
I
is the function of Definition
5.16
.

(2)

In the complete structure on
M
, each component of
\Sigma
has length at most
0.0996,
while the total length of
\Sigma
is
\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)
, where
{\operatorname{haze}}
is the function of Remark
4.23
.

Then we have the double-sided inequality

\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}+\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}}<\ell<\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}},

where
G
and
\widetilde{G}
are defined in Lemma
6.7
.

Furthermore,
M-\Sigma
and
M
are connected by a cone deformation maintaining a multi-tube about
\Sigma
of radius
R
, where
\tanh R=Z>{Z_{\min}}
throughout.

Proof.

If (
1
) holds, Theorem
5.17
proves the existence of a cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
that maintains
Z>{Z_{\min}}
throughout.

If (
2
) holds, then Lemma
5.15
implies that the maximal tube about
\Sigma
has radius
R>0.531
. Furthermore, since
\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)=h({R_{\min}})/(2\pi)
, Theorem
5.14
proves the existence of a cone deformation from
M
to
M-\Sigma
that maintains
Z>{Z_{\min}}
throughout.

Applying Lemma
6.8
with
\alpha\leq 2\pi
gives

(6.11)

L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}\><\>u\><\>L^{2}+\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}.

Now, we substitute
u=\alpha/\ell
and
\alpha=2\pi
, obtaining

(6.12)

L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}\><\>\frac{2\pi}{\ell}\><\>L^{2}+\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}.

We need to make sure that the lower bound on
2\pi/\ell
is strictly positive, to invert the three quantities in (
6.12
).

If
L^{2}\geq I({Z_{\min}})
with
{Z_{\min}}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
, then
L^{2}\geq I(1/\sqrt{3})>57.504
by the monotonicity of
I
; see Definition
5.16
. Meanwhile,
G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}\leq G(1/\sqrt{3})<40.274
, hence the lower bound is positive in this case.

If
\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)
, the second inequality in (
6.12
) ensures that

L^{2}>(2\pi)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\right),

hence

L^{2}-G({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}>(2\pi)^{2}\left(\frac{1}{{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})-G({Z_{\min}})\right).

The right hand side is positive when
{Z_{\min}}=0.6622
.
Using the fact that the functions
{\operatorname{haze}}
,
G
, and
\widetilde{G}
are all strictly decreasing for
Z>{Z_{\min}}
(Remark
4.23
and Lemma
6.7
), it follows that the left hand side is also positive for
Z\geq{Z_{\min}}\geq 0.6622
.

Thus all terms in (
6.12
) are positive, and we can take the reciprocal of each term. Solving for
\ell
completes the proof.
∎

We will apply Lemma
6.10
to obtain relations between
L
and
\ell
. The following is the simplest and most exportable version of the lemma, using
{Z_{\min}}=0.6624
.

Corollary 6.13
.

Suppose that
M
is a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
is a geodesic link such that one of the following hypotheses holds.

(1)

In the complete structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
is
L\geq 7.823
.

(2)

In the complete structure on
M
, each component of
\Sigma
has length at most
0.0996
and the total length of
\Sigma
is
\ell\leq 0.1396
.

Then

\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}+16.17}<\ell<\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-28.78}.

6.3.
Boundary terms for general tubes

The following proposition gives an explicit bound on boundary terms along a general tube about
\Sigma
. This bound will be used in Section
7
to control the change in length of a short non-singular geodesic. Versions of Proposition
6.14
with stronger hypotheses (see Theorem
6.20
) will also be used in the bilipschitz estimates of Section
8
.

Proposition 6.14
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold, and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
.
Let
M_{t}
be a cone-manifold occurring along a deformation between
M-\Sigma
and
M
, as in Theorem
5.1
.

Let
U_{\mathbf{r}}(\Sigma)
be an embedded (not necessarily maximal) multi-tube about the cone locus
\Sigma
.
Suppose the smallest radius of a tube is
r
, and let
z=\tanh r
. Suppose that the area of each tube boundary is at least
A
.

Suppose that in the complete structure on
M
, each component of
M
has length at most
0.0996
and the total length of
\Sigma
is
\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)
, where
{Z_{\min}}\geq 0.6622
and
{\operatorname{haze}}
is the function of Remark
4.23
. Then

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\leq\frac{1}{4Az(3-z^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\ell}\right)^{2},

where
\widetilde{G}
is as in Lemma
6.7
. In particular, if
\ell\leq 0.075
, then

b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\leq\frac{1}{4Az(3-z^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-12.355\ell}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

We compute as follows:

\displaystyle b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\frac{4(1-z_{j}^{2})}{z_{j}^{2}(3-z_{j}^{2})}\cdot\frac{1}{16\alpha^{4}}\mathcal{A}_{j}\qquad\mbox{ by Lemma~\ref{Lem:hkshape5.4}}

\displaystyle=\sum\frac{1}{4\mathcal{A}_{j}}\cdot\frac{1-z_{j}^{2}}{z_{j}^{2}(3-z_{j}^{2})}\mathcal{A}_{j}^{2}\cdot 16\,\cdot\frac{1}{16\alpha^{4}}\cdot\frac{\sinh r_{j}\cosh r_{j}}{\sinh r_{j}\cosh r_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum\frac{1}{4\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{j})}\cdot\frac{1-z_{j}^{2}}{z_{j}^{2}(3-z_{j}^{2})}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{A}_{j}^{2}}{\alpha^{4}}\cdot\frac{z_{j}}{1-z_{j}^{2}}\quad\mbox{ by \eqref{Eqn:TorusArea} and Lemma~\ref{Lem:TanhSinhCosh}}

\displaystyle=\sum\frac{1}{4\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{j})}\cdot\frac{1}{z_{j}(3-z_{j}^{2})}\cdot v_{j}^{2}\qquad\mbox{ where }v_{j}=\mathcal{A}_{j}/\alpha^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\frac{1}{4A}\cdot\frac{1}{z(3-z^{2})}\cdot v_{j}^{2}\qquad\mbox{using monotonicity of $(z(3-z^{2}))^{-1}$}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4A}\cdot\frac{1}{z(3-z^{2})}\cdot v^{2}\qquad\qquad\mbox{where }v=\sum v_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4A}\cdot\frac{1}{z(3-z^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)^{2}\qquad\mbox{where }u=1/v.

Now, observe that under our hypotheses,
Lemma
6.10

ensures a cone deformation between cone angle
\alpha
and cone angle
2\pi
for which the
\tanh
of the maximal tube stays bounded below by

{Z_{\min}}\geq 0.6622
.
Thus, by Lemma
6.9
,

u\geq\frac{2\pi}{\ell}-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\cdot(2\pi)^{2}=\frac{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\,\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\,\ell}{\ell}.

Note that the right hand side is positive, because
\ell\leq h({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)
and
{Z_{\min}}>0.6622
implies
2\pi/\ell-\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})(2\pi)^{2}\geq 28.8>0
.
Now, we may invert the lower bound on
u
to obtain the desired upper bound on
b_{\mathbf{r}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})
.

In the specific case
\ell<0.075\leq h(0.8477)/(2\pi)
, Lemma
6.10
ensures the cone deformation exists with
{Z_{\min}}>0.8477
. Substituting that value into
\widetilde{G}(z)
gives the bound.
∎

6.4.
Boundary terms along thin tubes

We close this section by establishing certain versions of Proposition
6.14
in the specific situation where the multi-tube
U_{\mathbf{r}}
is defined by a small injectivity radius. See Theorem
6.20
for a detailed statement.

This result will be used to prove bilipschitz estimates; see Theorem
8.3
and Theorem
8.18
in Section
8
.
The conclusion that a multi-tube
U_{\mathbf{r}}
has a certain depth will also prove crucial in controlling Margulis numbers in Section
9
. On the other hand, the results of this subsection are not needed in Section
7
. Thus, a reader who is mainly interested in the application to cosmetic surgeries can skip ahead to Section
7
.

Definition 6.15
.

Let
U
be a tube about a component of
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
. For
\delta>0
, we say that
U
is a
\delta
–thin tube
if
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U)=\delta/2
for a point
x\in\partial U
. (Recall Definition
2.17
.) We emphasize that the term
\delta
–thin tube
refers only to injectivity radius in
U
, not in all of
M
.

Lemma 6.16
.

Fix
0<\delta<0.9623
.
Supppose
M
is a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
, while the total length of
\Sigma
is

(6.17)

\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\leq\min\left\{0.261\delta,\>\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)\right\},

where
{\operatorname{haze}}
is defined in Remark
4.23
.
Then
M-\Sigma
is connected to
M
via a cone deformation
M_{t}
, while maintaining a multi-tube of radius
R\geq h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)\geq 0.7555
.

Fix a cone-manifold
M_{t}
in the interior of the deformation. For each component
\sigma_{j}
, let
r_{j}(\delta)=r_{\alpha,\lambda,\tau}(\delta)
be the tube radius of the
\delta
–thin tube about
\sigma_{j}
in the metric
g_{t}
. Set

{\mathbf{r}}(\delta)=(r_{1}(\delta),\ldots,r_{n}(\delta)).

Then

(1)

For every
j
, we have
r_{j}(\delta)>1.001(\delta/2)
.

(2)

The multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}(\delta)}
is embedded in
M_{t}
. Furthermore, each
\delta
-thin tube of radius
r_{j}(\delta)
is properly contained in a component of the maximal tube
U_{\max}
.

Conclusion (
1
) can be interpreted as follows:
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U)
is realized by a ball
B=B_{\delta/2}(\widetilde{x})
bumping into another translate of
B
, and furthermore the bumping does not occur along the singular locus. The translate is somewhere else.

See Figure
6.1
for a graph of the upper bound on
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)
. Roughly speaking, the first hypothesis
\ell\leq 0.261\delta
corresponds to conclusion (
1
), while the second hypothesis
2\pi\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)
corresponds to conclusion (
2
). Both conclusions also require
\ell\leq 0.1453
, where
0.1453
is (approximately) the peak of the graph in Figure
6.1
.

Proof of Lemma
6.16
.

We begin by analyzing the two functions whose minimum is the bound on
\ell
in (
6.17
). Recall from Lemma
4.22

that
\frac{1}{2\pi}h(r)=\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}(\tanh r)
has a single critical point for
r>0
, and that this critical point is a global maximum. As a consequence, it is an easy exercise to check that the two functions of (
6.17
) intersect exactly once, at

\delta=\delta_{\rm cut}=0.556369\ldots

Define

R_{\rm cut}=h^{-1}(2\pi\cdot 0.261\delta_{\rm cut})=\arctanh\left(\frac{\delta_{\rm cut}+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)=0.75552\ldots,

and note that
\tanh R_{\rm cut}>1/\sqrt{3}=0.57735\ldots
. This helps us analyze the two functions.

\begin{overpic}[DeltaEmbeds.png]
\put(25.0,40.0){$0.261\delta$}
\put(83.0,40.0){$\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)}$}
\put(51.0,-4.0){$\delta$}
\put(-2.0,32.0){$\ell$}
\put(60.0,59.0){$\bullet\,(\delta_{\rm cut},0.261\delta_{\rm cut})$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 6.1.
The function of
\delta
that provides an upper bound on
\ell
in Equation (
6.17
).

If
0<\delta<\delta_{\rm cut}
, then the minimum in (
6.17
) is achieved by
0.261\delta
, an increasing linear function. See Figure
6.1
, left.
On the other hand, if
\delta_{\rm cut}\leq\delta<0.9623
, then the minimum is achieved by

\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}(z)
, where

z=\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\geq\frac{\delta_{\rm cut}+0.1604}{1.1227}>\sqrt{\sqrt{5}-2}.

In particular,
z
is large enough that the function
\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}(z)
is decreasing in
z
by Lemma
4.22
and Remark
4.23
,
hence decreasing in
\delta
. See Figure
6.1
, right. Thus the largest possible upper bound on
\ell
occurs at
\delta=\delta_{\rm cut}
.

We conclude that for all values
0<\delta<0.9623
, Equation (
6.17
) requires the visual area of
\Sigma
to satisfy

\mathcal{A}=2\pi\ell\leq 2\pi\cdot 0.261\delta_{\rm cut}=h(R_{\rm cut})\leq 2\pi\cdot 0.14522.

Furthermore,
under our hypotheses on
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})
, Lemma
5.15
implies that the maximal tube about
\Sigma
has radius
R>0.531
.
It follows that Theorem
5.14
guarantees a cone deformation from

M
to
M-\Sigma
, maintaining a multi-tube
U_{\max}
of radius

R\geq{R_{\min}}=h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)\geq R_{\rm cut}.

where the last inequality uses the decreasing property of
h^{-1}
.

We are now ready to prove conclusion (
2
). Consider a component
U_{j}\subset U_{\max}
, of radius
R_{j}
, and recall that

R_{j}\geq{R_{\min}}\geq R_{\rm cut}>\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
. Define
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}
, and
Z_{\rm cut}=\tanh R_{\rm cut}
, as usual. Theorem
4.26
says that for every
x\in\partial U_{j}
, we have

2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{j})>1.1227{Z_{\min}}-0.1604.

If
0<\delta\leq\delta_{\rm cut}
, we have

1.1227{Z_{\min}}-0.1604\geq 1.1227\,Z_{\rm cut}-0.1604=\delta_{\rm cut}\geq\delta,

Meanwhile, if
\delta_{\rm cut}\leq\delta<0.9623
,
Equation (
6.17
) and the decreasing property of
{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}
imply

1.1227{Z_{\min}}-0.1604=1.1227\,{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi\ell)-0.1604\geq\delta.

Thus, in either case, we can combine the above equations to conclude

(6.18)

2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(x,U_{j})>1.1227\,{Z_{\min}}-0.1604\geq\delta,

which implies
r_{j}(\delta)<{R_{\min}}\leq R_{j}
.
This means each component of
U_{{\mathbf{r}}(\delta)}
is properly contained in the corresponding component of
U_{\max}
.

To prove conclusion (
1
), let
\mathcal{A}_{j}=\alpha\lambda_{j}
be the visual area of
\sigma_{j}
, as in Definition
4.18
. Recall that by Lemma
5.9
,
\mathcal{A}=\sum\mathcal{A}_{j}
increases as the cone angle increases. At the complete structure, we have

\mathcal{A}=2\pi\ell\leq 2\pi\cdot 0.261\delta\leq 1.64\,\delta.

Thus, for every intermediate cone-manifold
M_{t}
, we also have

\mathcal{A}_{j}\leq\mathcal{A}\leq 1.64\,\delta
.
Since
\alpha<2\pi
, Lemma
3.4
applies to give

(6.19)

r_{j}(\delta)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\delta^{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{j}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\delta^{2}}{1.64\,\delta}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(1.056\,\delta\right).

Now suppose that
0<\delta\leq\delta_{\rm cut}
. Consider the secant line for
\arcsinh(1.056\,\delta)
between
\delta=0
and
\delta=\delta_{\rm cut}
; this line has slope
\arcsinh(1.056\delta_{\rm cut})/\delta_{\rm cut}>1.002
. By calculus,
\arcsinh(1.056\,\delta)
is strictly increasing and concave down for
\delta>0
, hence it lies over its secant line when
0<\delta\leq\delta_{\rm cut}
. Thus, in this range we have

r_{j}(\delta)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(1.056\,\delta\right)\geq 1.002\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right).

Meanwhile, if
\delta_{\rm cut}\leq\delta\leq 0.9623
,
then we still have
\mathcal{A}\leq 1.64\,\delta_{\rm cut}
at the complete structure, hence

\mathcal{A}_{j}\leq\mathcal{A}\leq 1.64\,\delta_{\rm cut}
at every intermediate cone-manifold. Thus we have the following analogue of (
6.19
):

r_{j}(\delta_{\rm cut})\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\delta_{\rm cut}^{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{j}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\delta_{\rm cut}^{2}}{1.64\,\delta_{\rm cut}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(1.002\,\delta_{\rm cut}\right).

Thus by Lemma
3.1
,

r_{j}(\delta)\>\geq\>r_{j}(\delta_{\rm cut})+\frac{\delta-\delta_{\rm cut}}{2}\>\geq\>\frac{1}{2}\big(1.002\,\delta_{\rm cut}+(\delta-\delta_{\rm cut})\big)\>>\>\frac{1}{2}\left(1.001\,\delta\right),

where the second inequality is the above lower bound on
r_{j}(\delta_{\rm cut})
, and the third inequality holds because
\delta
is less than twice as big as
\delta_{\rm cut}
.
∎

Now, we can combine Proposition
6.14
and Lemma
6.16
to control the boundary terms along certain thin tubes.

Theorem 6.20
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Let
\ell
denote the length of
\Sigma
in the complete structure on
M
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq{\delta_{\max}}\leq 0.938
, and suppose that
\ell\leq\delta^{2}B(\delta)
, where
B(\delta)
is a nondecreasing function of
\delta
, with
B(\delta)\leq 1/17.11
. (In particular, this assumption implies
\ell\leq 0.05143
.)

Fix a cone-manifold
M_{t}
in the interior of the cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, with associated cone-metric
g_{t}
. For each component
\sigma_{j}
of
\Sigma
, let
r_{j}(\delta)=r_{\alpha,\lambda,\tau}(\delta)
be the tube radius of the
\delta
–thin tube about
\sigma_{j}
in the metric
g_{t}
.
Define

{\mathbf{r}}_{-}={\mathbf{r}}_{-}(\delta)=(r_{1}(\delta)-\delta/2,\ldots,r_{n}(\delta)-\delta/2).

Then

(1)

For all
j=1,\dots,n
,

r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi B({\delta_{\max}})}\right)-\frac{{\delta_{\max}}}{2}\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3}).

(2)

The multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
is embedded in
M_{t}
.

(3)

The boundary term along the tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
satisfies

b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\left(\frac{\ell}{7.935\,\delta}\right)^{2}.

Proof.

We start by proving (
2
). We will do this by applying Lemma
6.16
to a multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)}
for a certain value
\gamma<\delta
.
Specifically, define
\gamma>0
so that

\cosh\gamma-1=\frac{\cosh\delta-1}{\cosh^{2}(\delta/2)},\quad\mbox{ i.e.\ }\quad\gamma=\arccosh\left(\frac{\cosh\delta-1}{\cosh^{2}(\delta/2)}+1\right).

Observe that
\gamma
is strictly increasing in
\delta
on
(0,0.938)
, and that
\gamma<\delta
in this range. Moreover, as
\delta
approaches
0
,
\gamma
approaches
0
, and as
\delta
approaches
0.938
,
\gamma
approaches
\gamma_{\max}=0.84904\ldots
.

Consider a model solid torus
N_{j}=N_{\alpha,\lambda,\tau}
such that a neighborhood of
\sigma_{j}
is modeled on
N_{j}
(compare Definition
2.4
). By Theorem
3.6
, the radii of
\gamma
–thin and
\delta
–thin tori in
N_{j}
satisfy

r_{j}(\delta)-r_{j}(\gamma)\leq\arccosh\sqrt{\frac{\cosh\delta-1}{\cosh\gamma-1}}=\frac{\delta}{2},

where the equality holds by the definition of
\gamma
.

With an eye toward Lemma
6.16
, we claim that our hypotheses imply

(6.21)

\ell\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{17.11}\leq\min\left\{0.261\gamma,\>\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\gamma+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)\right\}.

The first inequality holds by hypothesis.
For the next inequality
\delta^{2}/17.11\leq 0.261\gamma
, consider the function
g(\delta)=(0.261\cdot 17.11)\gamma-\delta^{2}
.
One can show by calculus
2
2

2

The second derivative of
\gamma
is negative, so the same is true for the second derivative of
g
. Thus the minimum of
g^{\prime}
occurs at
\delta_{\max}
, and this minimum is positive.
that
g
is strictly increasing for
0<\delta<\delta_{\max}
. The minimum value of
g
thus occurs as
\delta\to 0
, and we have
g(\delta)>g(0)=0
on this domain.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.16
, we need to verify the remaining inequality only for
\delta_{\rm cut}<\gamma<\gamma_{\max}
. On this domain,
\delta^{2}
is strictly increasing whereas
{\operatorname{haze}}(\cdot)
is strictly decreasing, by Lemma
4.22
. Thus it suffices to plug in the maximal
\delta
-value
0.938
, which corresponds to
\gamma_{\max}=0.84904\ldots
. Plugging in these values of
\delta
and
\gamma
gives

\frac{(0.938)^{2}}{17.11}=0.05142\ldots<0.05147\ldots=\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\gamma_{\max}+0.1604}{1.1227}\right),

hence (
6.21
) holds for all
\delta\leq{\delta_{\max}}\leq 0.938
.
Thus, by Lemma
6.16
, the multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)}
of radius
{\mathbf{r}}(\gamma)
is embedded. Since
r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2\leq r_{j}(\gamma)
for every
j
, it follows that the multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}(\delta)}
is embedded as well.

The proof of condition (
1
) is similar to the corresponding argument in Lemma
6.16
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{j}=\alpha\lambda_{j}
be the visual area of
\sigma_{j}
. By Lemma
5.9
,
\mathcal{A}=\sum\mathcal{A}_{j}
increases as the cone angle increases.
Since
\mathcal{A}=2\pi\ell\leq 2\pi\delta^{2}B(\delta)

at the complete structure on
M
, we also have
\mathcal{A}_{j}\leq 2\pi\delta^{2}B(\delta)

at every intermediate cone-manifold
M_{t}
.
Since
\alpha<2\pi
, Lemma
3.4
applies to give

(6.22)

r_{j}(\delta)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}\,\delta^{2}}{\mathcal{A}_{j}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi B(\delta)}\right)\geq 1.1276\ldots.

Since
\delta\leq{\delta_{\max}}
and
B(\delta)^{-1}\geq 17.11
, we conclude that

r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2\geq\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi B(\delta)}\right)-\frac{{\delta_{\max}}}{2}>0.65847\ldots=\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3}),

proving (
1
).

It remains to bound
b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)
, establishing conclusion (
3
). Since the smallest coordinate of
{\mathbf{r}}_{-}
is larger than
\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3}
), Lemma
6.4
and (
6.3
) tell us that
b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})
.

We will bound
b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})
using Proposition
6.14
.
By (
4.19
) and Lemma
3.3
, the torus
T^{\delta}=T_{r_{j}(\delta)}
has area

\operatorname{area}(T_{r_{j}(\delta)})=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{j}}{2}\sinh(2r_{j}(\delta))\geq\sqrt{3}\delta^{2}/2.

To apply Proposition
6.14
, we need a lower bound on the area of each boundary torus of
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
, where the
j
-th torus has radius
r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2
. This can be computed using Lemma
3.3
and Lemma
A.3
.
Note that by hypothesis,
\delta\leq{\delta_{\max}}\leq 0.938
. By (
6.22
), we have

\tanh(2r_{j}(\delta))\geq\tanh\left(\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi B({\delta_{\max}})}\right)\right)=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{3+4\pi^{2}B({\delta_{\max}})^{2}}}=:z_{\min}.

Observe that
\tanh(\delta_{\max})=0.7343\ldots
and
z_{\min}\geq 0.9782\ldots
, hence
\tanh(\delta_{\max})\leq z_{\min}\leq\tanh(2r_{j}(\delta))
, and Lemma
A.3
applies with
r=2r_{j}(\delta)
,
s=\delta
, and
z_{\min}
as above.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{area}(T_{r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{j}}{2}\sinh(2r_{j}(\delta)-\delta)

by (
4.19
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{j}}{2}\sinh(2r_{j}(\delta))\left(\cosh(\delta_{\max})-\frac{\sinh(\delta_{\max})}{z_{\min}}\right)

by Lemma
A.3

\displaystyle=\operatorname{area}(T_{r_{j}(\delta)})\left(\cosh({\delta_{\max}})-\frac{\sinh({\delta_{\max}})}{{z_{\min}}}\right)

by (
4.19
)

\displaystyle{\geq\delta^{2}\,\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\left(\cosh({\delta_{\max}})-\frac{\sinh({\delta_{\max}})\sqrt{3+4\pi^{2}B({\delta_{\max}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)}

(6.23)

\displaystyle=:\delta^{2}\,A_{\mathrm{Bd}}({\delta_{\max}},B({\delta_{\max}})).

We may now finish the proof using Proposition
6.14
. Recall that the function
(z(3-z^{2}))^{-1}
is monotonically decreasing on
(0,1)
,
where
z=\tanh(r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2)
in our setting. By conclusion (
1
), we obtain

(6.24)

z\geq\tanh\left(\frac{1}{2}\arcsinh\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi B({\delta_{\max}})}\right)-\frac{{\delta_{\max}}}{2}\right)=:{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}({\delta_{\max}},B({\delta_{\max}}))\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}.

Recall that

\ell\leq{\delta_{\max}}^{2}B({\delta_{\max}})\leq{\delta_{\max}}^{2}/17.11<0.05143
.
Thus we also have
\ell\leq{\operatorname{haze}}({Z_{\min}})/(2\pi)
for

{Z_{\min}}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi{\delta_{\max}}^{2}B({\delta_{\max}}))\geq 0.8992
.
By Proposition
6.14
,

\displaystyle b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\,

\displaystyle b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4A}\cdot\frac{1}{z(3-z^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\,\ell}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\,\delta^{2}A_{\mathrm{Bd}}}\cdot\frac{1}{{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}(3-{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}^{2})}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\widetilde{G}({Z_{\min}})\,{\delta_{\max}}^{2}B({\delta_{\max}})}\right)^{2}.

In the particular case that
{\delta_{\max}}=0.938
and
B(\delta)=1/17.11
, we obtain

\displaystyle b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\,\delta^{2}\cdot 0.3181}\cdot\frac{1}{(1/\sqrt{3})(3-1/3)}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\widetilde{G}(0.8992)\cdot(0.05143)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\ell}{7.935\,\delta}\right)^{2}.\qed

Remark 6.25
.

In the course of proving Theorem
6.20
, we actually proved something more general. We showed that

b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\frac{1}{4\cdot\delta^{2}\,A_{\mathrm{Bd}}}\cdot\frac{1}{{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}\left(3-{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}^{2}\right)}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-4\pi^{2}\widetilde{G}\left({Z_{\min}}\right){\delta_{\max}}^{2}B({\delta_{\max}})}\right)^{2},

where
A_{\mathrm{Bd}}({\delta_{\max}},B({\delta_{\max}}))
is defined in (
6.23
), and
{z_{\mathrm{B}d}}({\delta_{\max}},B({\delta_{\max}}))
is defined in (
6.24
),
and
\widetilde{G}
is the function of Lemma
6.7
with
{Z_{\min}}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi{\delta_{\max}}^{2}B({\delta_{\max}}))
.

Our applications often require much stronger bounds on
\delta
and
B(\delta)
than the maximum values allowed in Theorem
6.20
. As a consequence, we can obtain better bounds on
b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)
. For example, the following two stronger estimates will be useful in Section
9
.

Proposition 6.26
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
, with
\ell
the length of
\Sigma
in the complete structure on
M
.
Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.106
. Suppose
\ell\leq\delta^{5/2}/17.49
.

Fix a cone-manifold
M_{t}
in the interior of the cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
. Define
{\mathbf{r}}_{-}=(r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2)
, and construct the multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
about
\Sigma

exactly as in Theorem
6.20
.
Then

(1)

r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2\geq\arctanh(0.9277)
for all
j
.

(2)

The multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
is embedded in
M_{t}
.

(3)

The boundary term along the tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
satisfies

b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\left(\ell/(15.616\,\delta)\right)^{2}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
6.20
and Remark
6.25
, letting
{\delta_{\max}}=0.106
and
B(\delta)=\sqrt{\delta}/17.49
.
∎

Proposition 6.27
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
, with
\ell
the length of
\Sigma
in the complete structure on
M
.
Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.012
. Suppose
\ell\leq\delta^{5/2}/16.62
.

Fix a cone-manifold
M_{t}
in the interior of the cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
. Define
{\mathbf{r}}_{-}=(r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2)
, and construct the multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
about
\Sigma

exactly as in Theorem
6.20
.
Then

(1)

r_{j}(\delta)-\delta/2\geq\arctanh(0.9760)
for all
j
.

(2)

The multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
is embedded in
M_{t}
.

(3)

The boundary term along the tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
satisfies

b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\left(\ell/(16.432\,\delta)\right)^{2}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
6.20
and Remark
6.25
, letting
{\delta_{\max}}=0.012
and
B(\delta)=\sqrt{\delta}/16.62
.
∎

7.
Short geodesics in a cone-manifold

The primary goal of this section is to control the complex length of a short geodesic during a cone deformation. Ineffective control of this type was previously shown by Bromberg
[
14
, Theorem 1.4]
. Following the theme of this paper, we combine some ideas in Bromberg’s argument (specifically,
[
14
, Proposition 4.3]
) with our estimates from earlier sections in order to obtain an effective estimate on the change in length under explicit hypotheses. Our results in this vein are incorporated in Theorem
7.19
(which provides control under hypotheses in the filled manifold) and Theorem
7.21
(which provides control under hypotheses in the cusped manifold).

One particular consequence of Theorem
7.19
is that for (explicitly quantified) long Dehn fillings of a cusped manifold
N
, the union of cores of the filling solid tori is shorter than any other closed geodesic in the filled manifold
M=N(\mathbf{s})
. See Theorem
7.28
. This tuple of shortest closed geodesics must be permuted by any isometry of
M
, providing an effective upper bound on the length of cosmetic fillings of a cusped
N
. As a consequence, we can prove the cosmetic surgery results that were stated in the introduction.

7.1.
Hyperbolic distance between lengths

The following notation will be valid throughout the section. As above, we have a one-parameter family of cone manifolds denoted
(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
or
M_{t}
for short. Let
\gamma
be a closed geodesic disjoint from
\Sigma
. We denote the complex length of
\gamma
in the cone-metric
g_{t}
by

\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma)=\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)+i\tau_{t}(\gamma),

where
\operatorname{len}_{t}
is the real length and
\tau_{t}
is the rotational component of
\gamma
.
When the choice of metric
g_{t}
is clear from context, we may drop the subscript
t
. All derivatives of
\mathcal{L}
are presumed to be with respect to the cone deformation parameter
t
.

Since
\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)>0
, the “rotated” complex length
i\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
is an element of the upper half-plane
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
, which we identify with the hyperbolic plane. As we will see in Lemma
7.4
, it is natural to control the change in complex length using the hyperbolic metric on
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
.

Definition 7.1
.

Given complex numbers
v
and
w
with positive real part, define the
hyperbolic distance

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(v,w)=d_{{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}(iv,\,iw).

The distance
{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}
has the following interpretation.
Consider a closed geodesic
\gamma
lying at the core curve of a (non-singular) model solid torus
N=N_{2\pi,\lambda,\tau}={\mathbb{H}}^{3}/\langle\varphi\rangle
. The cyclic group
\langle\varphi\rangle
has two fixed points
p_{+},p_{-}\in\partial{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, and acts by conformal covering transformations on
S^{2}-\{p_{\pm}\}
. The quotient torus
(S^{2}-\{p_{\pm}\})/\langle\varphi\rangle
inherits a conformal structure, which can be viewed as the
conformal boundary at infinity
, denoted
\partial_{\infty}N
. In the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(T^{2})
of conformal structures on a torus, the conformal boundary
\partial_{\infty}N
is the limit of the conformal structures on equidistant tori
T_{r}\subset N
.

The Teichmüller metric
d_{\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{T}(T^{2})
is isometric to
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
. Thus, given a pair of closed geodesics
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
, with complex lengths
\mathcal{L}(\gamma)=\lambda+i\tau
and
\mathcal{L}(\gamma^{\prime})=\lambda^{\prime}+i\tau^{\prime}
, we have

d_{\mathcal{T}}(\partial_{\infty}N_{2\pi,\lambda,\tau},\,\partial_{\infty}N_{2\pi,\lambda^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}})=d_{{\mathbb{H}}^{2}}(i\lambda-\tau,\,i\lambda^{\prime}-\tau^{\prime})={d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}(\gamma),\mathcal{L}(\gamma^{\prime})).

See Minsky
[
45
, Section 6.2]
, where this perspective is fleshed out further.

Definition 7.2
.

For
z\in(0,1)
and
\ell\in(0,0.5085)
, define a function

(7.3)

F(z,\ell)=\frac{(1+z^{2})}{z^{3}(3-z^{2})}\cdot\frac{\ell}{10.667-20.977\ell}\,.

Note that
F
is positive everywhere on its domain, decreasing in
z
, and increasing in
\ell
.

The following is an effective version of a result of Bromberg
[
14
, Proposition 4.3]
. Our proof follows Bromberg’s line of argument while inserting the explicit estimates of Section
6
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose
(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
is a cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, parametrized by
t\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
. Let
\gamma\subset M
be a simple closed curve disjoint from
\Sigma
, and let
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
. Let
[a,b]
be a sub-interval of
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
. Suppose that the following hold:

(1)

In the complete structure on
M
, the total length of
\Sigma
is
\ell\leq 0.075
.

(2)

For
t\in[a,b]
, the curve
\gamma
is a geodesic in the cone metric
g_{t}
.

(3)

For all
t\in[a,b]
, there is an embedded maximal multi-tube
U_{\max}(\Sigma^{+})
in the
g_{t}
metric, such that all constituent tubes have radius at least
{R_{\min}}
, where
{\hat{Z}_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
.

Then, for
t\in[a,b]
, the time derivative of the complex length
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma)
satisfies

\frac{|\mathcal{L}_{t}^{\prime}(\gamma)|}{\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)}\leq F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell),

where
F
is the function of Definition
7.2
. Consequently,

(7.5)

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{a}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{b}(\gamma))\leq|b-a|\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell).

Proof.

Let
U_{\gamma}
be the tube about
\gamma
in the maximal multi-tube
U_{\max}(\Sigma^{+})
.
By Theorem
5.6
, there is a local cone deformation on
M
that treats
\Sigma^{+}
as its singular locus but does not change the cone angle on
\gamma
. By the rigidity statement in Theorem
5.6
, the cone metric
g_{t}^{+}
on
(M,\Sigma^{+})
is entirely determined by the angles on
\Sigma
, hence it is isometric to the cone metric
g_{t}
in the statement of the lemma.
For the rest of the proof, we will not distinguish between
g_{t}
and
g_{t}^{+}
.

As in Section
5.1
, we may parametrize the infinitesimal deformation in
U_{\gamma}
by cylindrical coordinates. By equation (
5.4
), we find that the infinitesimal cone deformation in
U_{\gamma}
is given by

\omega=s(\gamma)\,\omega_{m}+(x+iy)\omega_{\ell}+\omega_{c},

where
\omega_{m}=\eta_{m}+i*D\eta_{m}
and
\omega_{\ell}=\eta_{\ell}+i*D\eta_{\ell}
are standard harmonic forms, with
\omega_{m}
giving infinitesimal change in cone angle, and
\omega_{\ell}
giving infinitesimal change in holonomy of the boundary of the tube
U_{\gamma}
but leaving the cone angle unchanged. The harmonic form
\omega_{c}
is a correction term, with real part
\eta_{c}
. The terms
s
,
x
, and
y
are real-valued functions of
t
. Moreover, recall from Theorem
5.6
that
s_{n+1}=s(\gamma)
determines the local change in cone angle at time
t
; since the cone angle about
\gamma
remains unchanged throughout the deformation,
s(\gamma)=0
. Thus, by (
5.5
), the function
x+iy
can be calculated to be:

x+iy=\frac{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\gamma)}{2\,\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)},

where
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma)
is the complex length of
\gamma
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\gamma)
is its time derivative, and
\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)
is the real length of
\gamma
at time
t
.

As in Section
6
, we
integrate over the submanifold
U_{\gamma}
.
Recall the definition of boundary terms from (
6.2
). By (
6.3
), we have

(7.6)
