\int_{U_{\gamma}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV=b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta,\eta)=b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})+b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{c},\eta_{c}).

Here,
\eta
is the real part of
\omega
and
\eta_{0}
is the real part of
(x+iy)\omega_{\ell}
. Meanwhile,
[
33
, Lemma 2.6]
implies that
b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{c},\eta_{c})\geq 0
. (This conclusion is the reverse of Lemma
6.4
because
\partial U_{\gamma}
is oriented by the inward normal, pointing toward
\gamma
.) Therefore, (
7.6
) implies

(7.7)

\int_{U_{\gamma}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV\geq b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0}).

Since
s(\gamma)=0
, the formulas in
[
33
, page 382]
imply

(7.8)

b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})=|x+iy|^{2}b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{\ell},\eta_{\ell})=\left(\frac{|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\gamma)|}{2\,\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}\right)^{2}b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{\ell},\eta_{\ell}).

An explicit formula for
b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{\ell},\eta_{\ell})
was computed in
[
33
, equation (13)]
:

(7.9)

\displaystyle b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{\ell},\eta_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sinh R_{\gamma}}{\cosh R_{\gamma}}\left(2+\frac{1}{\cosh^{2}R_{\gamma}}\right)\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{\gamma})

\displaystyle=Z_{\gamma}(3-Z_{\gamma}^{2})\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{\gamma})

(7.10)

\displaystyle\geq{Z_{\min}}(3-{Z_{\min}}^{2})\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{\gamma}).

Here,
R_{\gamma}
is the tube radius of
U_{\gamma}
, and
Z_{\gamma}=\tanh R_{\gamma}
as usual.
Note that (
7.9
) differs from the formula in
[
33
]
by a negative sign, again because
\partial U_{\gamma}
is oriented inward.
Thus, putting together equations (
7.7
), (
7.8
), and (
7.10
), we obtain

(7.11)

{Z_{\min}}(3-{Z_{\min}}^{2})\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{\gamma})\cdot\left(\frac{|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\gamma)|}{2\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}\right)^{2}\leq b_{U_{\gamma}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\leq\int_{U_{\gamma}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV.

Next, we will bound
\int\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV
using Proposition
6.14
. Let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{n}
be the components of
U_{\max}
whose cores are the geodesics of
\Sigma
. Let
\mathbf{R}=(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})
be the vector of radii of these tubes. Since
U_{\max}(\Sigma^{+})=U_{\mathbf{R}}(\Sigma)\cup U_{\gamma}
is an embedded multi-tube, we know that
U_{\mathbf{R}}
is also an embedded multi-tube, and
U_{\gamma}
is embedded in
M-U_{\mathbf{R}}
.

Since
\tanh R_{i}\geq{Z_{\min}}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
by hypothesis, we have the following estimate:

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\gamma}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M-U_{\mathbf{R}}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV

\displaystyle=b_{\mathbf{R}}(\eta,\eta)

\displaystyle\leq b_{\mathbf{R}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{0})\quad

(7.12)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\,A\,{Z_{\min}}(3-{Z_{\min}}^{2})}\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-12.355\,\ell}\right)^{2}.

Here, the first inequality uses the set containment
U_{\gamma}\subset M-U_{\mathbf{R}}
, the equality uses (
6.3
), the next inequality uses
Lemma
6.4
, and the final inequality uses Proposition
6.14
.
In (
7.12
),
recall that

\ell=\ell(\Sigma)
is the sum of the lengths of all components of
\Sigma
in the non-singular metric on
M
.
Meanwhile,
A
is any lower bound on the area of each torus
\partial U_{i}
. This area can be estimated using Theorem
4.16
:

(7.13)

\operatorname{area}(\partial U_{i})\geq A:=1.69785\,\frac{\sinh^{2}{R_{\min}}}{\cosh(2{R_{\min}})}=1.69785\,\frac{{Z_{\min}}^{2}}{1+{Z_{\min}}^{2}}\,.

Furthermore, Theorem
4.16
also implies that
\partial U_{\gamma}
satisfies the same lower bound.

Combining (
7.11
), (
7.12
), and (
7.13
) gives

\displaystyle\left(\frac{|\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\gamma)|}{2\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\,A^{2}\,{Z_{\min}}^{2}\big(3-{Z_{\min}}^{2}\big)^{2}}\left(\frac{\ell}{2\pi-12.355\,\ell}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+{Z_{\min}}^{2})^{2}}{2^{2}\,{Z_{\min}}^{6}\big(3-{Z_{\min}}^{2}\big)^{2}}\left(\frac{\ell}{1.69785\,(2\pi-12.355\,\ell)}\right)^{2},

which simplifies to the desired bound on
|\mathcal{L}_{t}^{\prime}(\gamma)|/\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)
.

It remains to prove (
7.5
). To that end, we offer the following interpretation.
The one-parameter family
i\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma)
is a curve in
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
. The speed with which this curve travels through
{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
(in the hyperbolic metric) is precisely
|\mathcal{L}_{t}^{\prime}(\gamma)|/\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)
. Since this speed is bounded by
F({Z_{\min}},\ell)
, integrating from
a
to
b
shows that
the hyperbolic distance between
\mathcal{L}_{a}(\gamma)
and
\mathcal{L}_{b}(\gamma)
is at most
|b-a|F({Z_{\min}},\ell)
.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\mathcal{L}(\gamma)=\operatorname{len}(\gamma)+i\tau(\gamma)
and
\mathcal{L}(\delta)=\operatorname{len}(\delta)+i\tau(\delta)
be complex lengths of geodesics satisfying
{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}(\delta))\leq K
for some
K>0
.
Then we have the following control on the real and imaginary parts of length:

(7.15)

e^{-K}\>\leq\>\frac{\operatorname{len}(\gamma)}{\operatorname{len}(\delta)}\>\leq\>e^{K},

(7.16)

|\tau(\gamma)-\tau(\delta)|\>\leq\>\sinh(K)\cdot\min\{\operatorname{len}(\gamma),\,\operatorname{len}(\delta)\}.

Proof.

Let
B_{K}
be the closed ball of hyperbolic radius
K
about
i\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
.
The top and bottom of this ball lie at Euclidean height
e^{\pm K}\operatorname{len}(\gamma)
from
\partial{\mathbb{H}}^{2}
.
Since the highest possible value of
\operatorname{len}(\delta)=\Im(i\mathcal{L}(\delta))
occurs at the highest point of
B_{K}
, and similarly for the lowest, we conclude that

e^{-K}\operatorname{len}(\gamma)\leq\operatorname{len}(\delta)\leq e^{K}\operatorname{len}(\gamma),

which is equivalent to (
7.15
).

To derive (
7.16
), observe that
|\tau(\delta)-\tau(\gamma)|
is at most the Euclidean radius of
B_{K}
. Since the highest and lowest points of
B_{K}
lie at height
e^{\pm K}\operatorname{len}(\gamma)
, it follows that

|\tau(\delta)-\tau(\gamma)|\leq\frac{e^{K}+e^{-K}}{2}\operatorname{len}(\gamma)=\sinh K\cdot\operatorname{len}(\gamma).

An identical argument, interchanging the roles of
\gamma
and
\delta
, yields
|\tau(\delta)-\tau(\gamma)|\leq\sinh K\cdot\operatorname{len}(\delta)
and completes the proof of (
7.16
).
∎

7.2.
The change in length

We can now show that the complex length of a short geodesic does not change too much under a cone deformation connecting
M-\Sigma
to
M
. We handle upward and downward cone deformations in two separate theorems.

To handle downward cone deformations, we need a lemma about the visual area of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 7.17
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Suppose that there is a cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
and maintaining an embedded tube about
\Sigma
of radius at least
R_{0}
, where
Z_{0}=\tanh R_{0}\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
.
Then the visual area of
\Sigma
in the
g_{t}
metric, denoted
\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma)
, satisfies

\frac{\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma)}{\mathcal{A}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)}\leq\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}\quad\text{where}\quad q(z)=\left(\frac{3z^{2}-1}{z^{2}(3-z^{2})}+1\right)\geq 1.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.9
,
\mathcal{A}_{t}=\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma)
satisfies the differential inequality

\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{t}}{dt}\geq\frac{\mathcal{A}_{t}}{2t}\left(\frac{3Z_{0}^{2}-1}{Z_{0}^{2}(3-Z_{0}^{2})}+1\right)=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{t}}{2t}\,q(Z_{0}),

and furthermore
q(z)\geq 1
for
z\geq 1/\sqrt{3}
. The above inequality can be rewritten

\frac{d\mathcal{A}_{t}}{\mathcal{A}_{t}}\geq\frac{q(Z_{0})}{2}\,\frac{dt}{t}.

Integration over the interval
[a,4\pi^{2}]
gives

\log\left(\frac{\mathcal{A}_{4\pi^{2}}}{\mathcal{A}_{a}}\right)\geq\frac{q(Z_{0})}{2}\,\log\left(\frac{4\pi^{2}}{a}\right)=\log\left(\left(\frac{4\pi^{2}}{a}\right)^{q(Z_{0})/2}\right),

which simplifies to the statement in the lemma after substituting
t=a
.
∎

In fact, we will actually need a bound on the visual area of
\Sigma\cup\gamma
, for
\gamma
a closed geodesic disjoint from
\Sigma
. The previous lemma and the following lemma together will give us the bound we need.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold,
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
a geodesic link in
M
,
\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)
and
m=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)
the lengths of
\Sigma
and
\gamma
in the complete metric on
M
.
Suppose
0\leq\ell\leq 0.0735
and
0\leq m\leq 0.0996-0.352\cdot\ell
.
Let

Z_{0}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi\ell)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{Z_{\min}}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}})),

where
{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}
is defined as in (
4.24
), and recall the functions
F
of Definition
7.2
and
q
of Lemma
7.17
.
Then the function

f(t)=f_{\ell,m}(t)=2\pi\ell\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}+2\pi m\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)F({Z_{\min}},\ell))

satisfies

f_{\ell,m}(t)<f_{\ell,m}(4\pi^{2})+2\pi\cdot{10^{-5}}=2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}}).

In the proof of Theorem
7.19
, we will see that
f_{\ell,m}(t)
serves as an upper bound on the total visual area
\mathcal{A}(\Sigma\cup\gamma)
in the
g_{t}
metric. Thus Lemma
7.18
will allow us to bound the visual area
\mathcal{A}(\Sigma\cup\gamma)
at time
t
in terms of the visual area at time
4\pi^{2}
. Graphing suggests that the inequality
f_{\ell,m}(t)\leq f_{\ell,m}(4\pi^{2})
holds without any error term, but the computer-assisted proof requires a (tiny) error term.

Proof of Lemma
7.18
.

Define an auxiliary function

g_{\ell,m}(t)=f_{\ell,m}(4\pi^{2})-f_{\ell,m}(t)=\int_{t}^{4\pi^{2}}f^{\prime}_{\ell,m}(x)\,dx.

Then the conclusion of the lemma can be phrased as
g_{\ell,m}(t)>-2\pi\cdot{10^{-5}}
for all values of
\ell,m,t
.

We claim that
g_{\ell,m}(t)
is smallest when
m
is largest. This can be seen from the derivative

f^{\prime}(t)=2\pi\ell\,\frac{q(Z_{0})}{2}\cdot\frac{t^{q(Z_{0})/2-1}}{(2\pi)^{q(Z_{0})}}-2\pi mF({Z_{\min}},\ell)\exp\big((4\pi^{2}-t)F({Z_{\min}},\ell)\big).

Observe that
{Z_{\min}}
is a decreasing function of
m
, while
F(z,\ell)
is a decreasing function of
z
. Thus
F({Z_{\min}},\ell)
is largest when
m
is largest. Hence the subtracted term in
f^{\prime}(t)
is largest when
m
is largest. Thus
f^{\prime}(t)
is smallest when
m
is largest, and the claim follows.

Now, we set
m=0.0996-0.352\cdot\ell
and claim that
g_{\ell,m}(t)>-2\pi\cdot{10^{-5}}
for all
\ell,t
with this value of
m
. This is established using interval arithmetic in Sage; see the ancillary files
[
23
]
.
∎

Theorem 7.19
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold.
Let
\Sigma
be a geodesic link in
M
, and
\gamma
a closed geodesic disjoint from
\Sigma
.
Let
\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)
and
m=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)
be the lengths of
\Sigma
and
\gamma
in the complete metric on
M
.
Suppose
\ell\leq 0.0735
and
m\leq 0.0996-0.352\cdot\ell
.
Let

{R_{\min}}=h^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}}))\geq\arctanh(0.6288).

Then
M-\Sigma
is connected to
M
via a cone deformation parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
, so that for all
t
,

(1)

The curve
\gamma
is a geodesic in the cone metric
g_{t}
. Furthermore, the cone deformation preserves
\gamma
setwise.

(2)

There is an embedded multi-tube about
\Sigma\cup\gamma
of radius greater than
{R_{\min}}
.

(3)

The complex length of
\gamma
satisfies

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma))\>\leq\>(4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell),

where
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}
and
F(z,\ell)
is the function of Definition
7.2
.

In particular, the length of
\gamma
in the
M
and
M-\Sigma
satisfies
{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma))\>\leq\>4\pi^{2}\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell)
.

Proof.

The proof is a crawling argument in the spirit of Theorem
5.14
. By that theorem, there is a cone deformation connecting
M-\Sigma
to
M
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
. Furthermore, this cone deformation maintains an embedded tube about
\Sigma
of radius at least
R_{0}=h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)
. (Note that for this proof, the lower bound on tube radius about
\Sigma
is denoted
R_{0}
rather than
{R_{\min}}
.)

Let
I
be the maximal sub-interval of
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
containing
(2\pi)^{2}
, such that conclusions (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) hold for
t\in I
.

First, we show that
(2\pi)^{2}\in I
, hence
I
is non-empty. Note that (
1
) holds by hypothesis, while (
3
) is vacuous for
t=(2\pi)^{2}
. Let
U_{\max}
be the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma^{+}
in the complete metric on
M
, and let
R
be the smallest radius of a tube in
U_{\max}
.
By Lemma
5.15
, we know
R>0.531
. Now, Corollary
4.25
says that

R\geq h^{-1}(\mathcal{A}(4\pi^{2}))=h^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+m))>{R_{\min}}

where the strict inequality is by definition of
{R_{\min}}
.
Thus (
2
) holds for
t=(2\pi)^{2}
, implying that
I\neq\emptyset
.

To see that
I
is open, let
0<t_{0}\in I
.
By Theorem
5.6
, there is a local cone deformation on
M
that treats
\Sigma^{+}
as its singular locus but does not change the cone angle on
\gamma
. Hence (
1
) holds in an open neighborhood of
t_{0}
, as does (
2
) because it is an open condition. Now, Lemma
7.4
implies that (
3
) holds on the union of
I
and this open neighborhood. Hence
I
is open.

To see that
I
is closed, let
a=\inf I
. Since the tube radius about
\Sigma^{+}
must remain
at least

{R_{\min}}
by continuity, and in particular does not degenerate, Theorem
5.13
implies that the cone deformation preserving
\gamma
setwise can be extended to
t=a
.
Since (
3
) is a closed condition, it holds at
t=a
by continuity. Thus, for
t\in[a,(2\pi)^{2}]
,
Lemma
7.14
gives

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{len}_{t}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)\cdot\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell))

\displaystyle=2\pi m\,\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell)).

Similarly, by Lemma
7.17
, the visual area of
\Sigma
satisfies

\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma)\>\leq\>\mathcal{A}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}\!\!=\>2\pi\ell\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}

where
Z_{0}=\tanh R_{0}
and the function
q(z)
is given in Lemma
7.17
. Combining the last two equations, we conclude that the total visual area of
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma^{+})

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\ell\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}\!\!+2\pi m\,\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell))=f_{\ell,m}(t).

By Lemma
7.18
, the function
f_{\ell,m}(t)
is bounded above in terms of
f_{\ell,m}(4\pi^{2})
.
In symbols,

\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma^{+})\leq f_{\ell,m}(t)<2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}})=h({R_{\min}}).

Thus Corollary
4.25
implies that at
t=a
, the maximal tube
U_{\max}
has smallest radius
R>{R_{\min}}
. This means condition (
2
) holds at
a=\inf I
, hence
I
is closed. Thus
(
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) hold for all
t\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
.
∎

Corollary 7.20
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold.
Let
\Sigma
be a geodesic link in
M
, and
\gamma
a closed geodesic disjoint from
\Sigma
.
Let
\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)
and
m=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)
be the lengths of
\Sigma
and
\gamma
in the complete metric on
M
. Suppose that
\max(\ell,m)\leq 0.0735
. Then
\gamma
is isotopic to a geodesic in the complete metric
g_{0}
on
M-\Sigma
, and furthermore

1.9793^{-1}\leq\frac{\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)}{\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)}\leq 1.9793\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\tau_{0}(\gamma)-\tau_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)|\leq 0.05417.

Proof.

First, observe that the hypothesis
m\leq 0.0735
implies
m\leq 0.0996-0.352\cdot\ell
when
0\leq\ell\leq 0.0735
. Thus our hypotheses are stronger than those of Theorem
7.19
.
Now, by Theorem
7.19
,

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma))\>\leq\>(4\pi^{2})\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell),

where
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}}))\geq 0.6299
.
Now, substitute

K=(2\pi)^{2}F({Z_{\min}},\ell)\leq 0.6827

into Lemma
7.14
, with the given bounds on
\ell
and
{Z_{\min}}
, and the given bound on
m
.
∎

We also have a version of Theorem
7.19
for upward cone deformations.

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Suppose that, in the complete hyperbolic structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies
L^{2}\geq 128
.
Let
\gamma\subset M-\Sigma
be a closed geodesic of length
m=\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)\leq 0.056
.
Define

{R_{\min}}=h^{-1}\left(\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{L^{2}-14.7}+2\pi\cdot 1.656\,m\right)>\arctanh(0.624).

Then
M-\Sigma
is connected to
M
via a cone deformation parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
, so that for all
t
,

(1)

The curve
\gamma
is a geodesic in the cone metric
g_{t}
. Furthermore, the cone deformation preserves
\gamma
setwise.

(2)

There is an embedded multi-tube about
\Sigma\cup\gamma
of radius greater than
{R_{\min}}
.

(3)

The complex length of
\gamma
satisfies

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{t}(\gamma))\>\leq\>t\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell),

where
\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}

and
F(z,\ell)
is as in Definition
7.2
.

In particular, the length of
\gamma
in the complete structures on
M
and
M-\Sigma
satisfies
{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma))\>\leq\>4\pi^{2}\,F\big({Z_{\min}},\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}\big)
.

As usual,
\ell
denotes the length of
\Sigma
in the hyperbolic metric on
M
. However, to apply the theorem, one only needs geometric hypotheses on
M-\Sigma
and the inequality
\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}
.

Proof.

We begin by noting that
L^{2}\geq 128\geq I(Z_{0})
, where
I(z)
is the function of Definition
5.16
and
Z_{0}\geq 0.8925>1/\sqrt{3}
. Thus, by Theorem
5.17
, there is a cone deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, parametrized by
t=\alpha^{2}
, for which the tube radius about
\Sigma
stays bounded below by
R_{0}
. (We denote this quantity by
R_{0}
rather than
{R_{\min}}
because
{R_{\min}}
has a different meaning in the present theorem.) Now, Lemma
6.10
, using
Z_{0}\geq 0.8925
, shows that the length of
\Sigma
in the complete metric on
M
is

(7.22)

\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-(2\pi)^{2}G(Z_{0})}\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}\leq 0.05546.

We will use this bound on
\ell
in applying Lemma
7.4
.

The rest of the proof is a crawling argument analogous to Theorem
7.19
.
By Theorem
5.6
and Theorem
5.13
, the cone deformation on
(M,\Sigma)
can be thought of as a cone deformation on
(M,\Sigma^{+})
, provided the tube radius about
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
does not degenerate. Thus conclusion (
1
) will be immediate once we establish (
2
).

Let
J
be the maximal sub-interval of
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, containing
0
, such that (
2
) and (
3
) both hold on
J
. By Lemma
5.15
and Corollary
4.25
, there is an embedded tube about
\gamma
in
M-\Sigma
of radius

R\geq h^{-1}(2\pi m)>{R_{\min}}.

Meanwhile, the horospherical cusp neighborhoods can be thought of as tubes of infinite radius about
\Sigma
. Thus (
2
) holds at
t=0
. Since (
3
) is vacuous at
t=0
, we conclude that
J
is non-empty.

The interval
J
is open for the same reason as in Theorem
7.19
. Let
t_{0}\in J
. Since (
2
) is an open condition, it holds on an open neighborhood about
t_{0}
. Now, Lemma
7.4
, combined with the estimate
(
7.22
), implies that (
3
) holds on the union of
J
and this open neighborhood. Hence
J
is open.

To see that
J
is closed, let
a=\sup J
. Since (
3
) is a closed condition, it holds at
t=a
by continuity. Thus, by Lemma
7.14
, we have

(7.23)

\operatorname{len}_{a}(\gamma)\leq\exp(a\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell))\cdot\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)<1.656\,m,

where the second inequality uses (
7.22
) and
{Z_{\min}}>0.624
, the fact that
F
is decreasing in
z
and increasing in
\ell
(since
\ell<0.056
), and the fact that
a\leq(2\pi)^{2}
.
Recall that
m=\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)
. Meanwhile, Lemma
5.9
implies that
\operatorname{len}_{t}(\Sigma)
is increasing in
t
. Thus, at time
t=a
,

\mathcal{A}(a)=\sqrt{a}\cdot\operatorname{len}_{a}(\Sigma)+2\pi\operatorname{len}_{a}(\gamma)<2\pi\cdot\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}+2\pi\cdot 1.656\,m=h({R_{\min}}).

Here, the first equality is by the definition of visual area, the inequality is by (
7.22
) and (
7.23
), and the second equality is by the definition of
{R_{\min}}
. Now, Corollary
4.25
implies
R\geq h^{-1}\mathcal{A}(a)>{R_{\min}}
, hence (
2
) holds at
t=a
as desired.
Therefore,
J
is closed, hence
J=[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
.
∎

Corollary 7.24
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Suppose that, in the complete hyperbolic structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies
L^{2}\geq 128
. Let
\gamma\subset M-\Sigma
be a closed geodesic of length
\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)\leq 0.056
. Then
\gamma
is isotopic to a closed geodesic in
M
, and furthermore

1.657^{-1}\leq\frac{\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)}{\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)}\leq 1.657\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\tau_{0}(\gamma)-\tau_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)|\leq 0.0295.

Proof.

Plug in
t=(2\pi)^{2}
,
\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.7}\leq\frac{2\pi}{113.3}
,
m\leq 0.056
and
{Z_{\min}}>0.624
into Lemma
7.14
to obtain the result.
∎

7.3.
Application to cosmetic surgery

We now present the main application of this section: effective control on cosmetic surgeries.

Definition 7.25
.

Choose a real number
L\geq 10.1
. Let
F
be the function of Definition
7.2
. Define

\ell_{\max}=\ell_{\max}(L)=\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-16.03}

and

{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)=\ell_{\max}\exp(4\pi^{2}F({\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\,\ell_{\max}+2\pi\,{10^{-5}}),\,\ell_{\max})).

Lemma 7.26
.

The function
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
is strictly decreasing in
L
. Furthermore, for
L\geq 10.1
,

\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}}<{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)<\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-58}.

Proof.

To see that
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
is decreasing, we examine the ingredients of its definition.
By Corollary
4.25
, and Remark
4.23
,
{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\ell_{\max}+2\pi{10^{-5}})
is a decreasing function of
\ell_{\max}
. By a derivative computation, the function

\frac{F(z,\ell)}{\ell}=\frac{(1+z^{2})}{z^{3}(3-z^{2})}\cdot\frac{1}{10.667-20.977\ell}

is decreasing in
z
and increasing in
\ell
. Thus
the combined function
F({\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\ell_{\max}+2\pi{10^{-5}}),\,\ell_{\max})
is increasing in
\ell_{\max}
. Since
\ell_{\max}=\ell_{\max}(L)
is strictly decreasing in
L
, we conclude that
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
is strictly decreasing.

The lower bound on
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
holds because

{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)>\ell_{\max}(L)>2\pi/L^{2}.

Meanwhile, by the definition of
\ell_{\max}
, the desired upper bound on
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
is equivalent to

\frac{{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)}{\ell_{\max}}\><\>\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-58}\cdot\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{2\pi}\,.

After substituting the definition of
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)
, taking logarithms, and dividing both sides by
\ell_{\max}
again, the upper bound becomes equivalent to the inequality

(7.27)

4\pi^{2}\,\frac{F({\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\ell_{\max}+2\pi{10^{-5}}),\,\ell_{\max})}{\ell_{\max}}\><\>\log\left(\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{L^{2}-58}\right)\cdot\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{2\pi}\,.

It remains to prove (
7.27
).

A derivative calculation shows that the right-hand side of (
7.27
) is decreasing in
L
. As
L\to\infty
, its limit is

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}-\log\left(\frac{L^{2}-58}{L^{2}-16.03}\right)\cdot\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}-\log\left(1-\frac{41.97}{L^{2}-16.03}\right)\cdot\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{41.97}{L^{2}-16.03}\cdot\frac{L^{2}-16.03}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\frac{41.97}{2\pi}=6.679\ldots.

Here, the second equality uses the linear approximation
\log(1-x)\sim-x
for
x
near
0
. Therefore, the right-hand side is at least
6.679
for all values of
L
in the domain.

Meanwhile, we have already checked that the left-hand side of (
7.27
) is increasing in
\ell_{\max}
, hence decreasing in
L
. Direct calculation shows that the left-hand side equals
6.674\ldots
when
L=11
. Thus inequality (
7.27
) holds for all
L\geq 11
.

Finally, for
L\in[10.1,11]
, inequality (
7.27
) is established using interval arithmetic in Sage. See the ancillary files
[
23
]
for details.
∎

Theorem 7.28
.

For a real number
L_{0}\geq 10.1
, let
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L_{0})
be the function of Definition
7.25
.
Let
N
be a cusped hyperbolic
3
–manifold whose systole is at least
{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L_{0})
. Let
\mathbf{s}
be a tuple of surgery slopes on the cusps of
N
, whose normalized length is
L=L(\mathbf{s})\geq L_{0}
.

Then the Dehn filled manifold
M=N(\mathbf{s})
is hyperbolic. The core
\Sigma
of the Dehn filling solid tori is isotopic to a geodesic link with an embedded tubular neighborhood of radius at least
1.281
.
Finally, the only geodesics in
M
of length at most
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)
are the components of
\Sigma
itself.

Proof.

Let
M=N(\mathbf{s})
, and let
\Sigma
be the union of the cores of the filled solid tori. We will apply Theorem
5.17
. Note the hypotheses imply that the normalized length is at least

L_{0}^{2}\geq(10.1)^{2}\geq I(0.8568),
where
I
is the function of Definition
5.16
.
By Theorem
5.17
,
M
admits a hyperbolic metric that is connected to the complete metric on
N=M-\Sigma
by a cone-deformation with singular locus along
\Sigma
. Moreover, the cone deformation maintains a tube about
\Sigma
of radius
R_{0}\geq\arctanh(0.8568)\geq 1.281
. At the end of this cone deformation,
\Sigma
becomes a geodesic link in the complete metric on
M
.
The length of
\Sigma
in this complete metric satisfies

\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)<\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-16.03}=\ell_{\max}<0.0731.

Here, the first inequality follows by Lemma
6.10
, plugging in the value
4\pi^{2}G(\tanh(R_{0}))=16.028\ldots
. Meanwhile, the last inequality uses the hypothesis
L\geq 10.1
.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
M
contains a closed geodesic
\gamma\not\subset\Sigma
such that
\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)=m\leq\ell_{\max}
. By Meyerhoff’s theorem
[
44
, Section 7]
,
\gamma\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
. Thus
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
is a geodesic link satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem
7.19
.
Matching the definition of
{R_{\min}}
in Theorem
7.19
,
we define

{Z_{\min}}={\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+m+{10^{-5}}))>{\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\ell_{\max}+2\pi\cdot{10^{-5}})>0.6337.

By Conclusion (
3
) of Theorem
7.19
, we have

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\gamma),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma))\>\leq\>4\pi^{2}\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell)\><\>4\pi^{2}\,F({Z_{\min}},\ell_{\max}),

hence Lemma
7.14
implies the length of
\gamma
in
M_{0}=M-\Sigma=N
is

\displaystyle\operatorname{len}_{0}(\gamma)

\displaystyle<\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\gamma)\,\exp(4\pi^{2}F({Z_{\min}},\ell_{\max}))

\displaystyle<\ell_{\max}\,\exp(4\pi^{2}F({\operatorname{haze}}^{-1}(4\pi\ell_{\max}+2\pi{10^{-5}}),\ell_{\max}))

\displaystyle={\operatorname{sysmin}}(L)\>\leq\>{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L_{0}),

using the fact that
F(z,\ell)
is decreasing in
z
.
But this contradicts the hypothesis that
{\operatorname{sys}}(N)\geq{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L_{0})
. This contradiction implies that the components of
\Sigma
are the
only
geodesics in
M
of length less than
\ell_{\max}
, completing the proof.
∎

Now, Theorem
7.28
combined with topological rigidity of hyperbolic manifolds
[
27
,
28
]
implies

Theorem 7.29
.

Let
N
be a cusped hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Suppose that
\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2}
are distinct tuples of slopes on the cusps of
N
, where the normalized length of each
\mathbf{s}_{i}
satisfies

L(\mathbf{s}_{i})\geq\max\left\{10.1,\,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{{\operatorname{sys}}(N)}+58}\right\}.

Then any homeomorphism
\varphi\colon N(\mathbf{s}_{1})\to N(\mathbf{s}_{2})
restricts (after an isotopy) to a self-homeomorphism of
N
sending
\mathbf{s}_{1}
to
\mathbf{s}_{2}
.
In particular, if
{\operatorname{sys}}(N)\geq 0.1428
, then the above conclusions hold for all pairs
(\mathbf{s}_{1},\mathbf{s}_{2})
of normalized length at least
10.1
.

Proof.

For
i=1,2
, let
L_{i}=L(\mathbf{s}_{i})
be the normalized length of the tuple of slopes
\mathbf{s}_{i}
. By hypothesis,
L_{i}\geq 10.1
and
L_{i}\geq\sqrt{2\pi/{\operatorname{sys}}(N)+58}
. Combining these hypotheses with Lemma
7.26
, we have

{\operatorname{sysmin}}(L_{i})\leq{\operatorname{sysmin}}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{{\operatorname{sys}}(N)}+58}\right)<{\operatorname{sys}}(N).

Let
\Sigma_{i}\subset N(\mathbf{s}_{i})
be the union of cores of the Dehn filling solid tori. Then, by Theorem
7.28
, the
k
components of
\Sigma_{i}
are the shortest
k
-tuple of geodesics in the hyperbolic manifold
N(s_{i})
. Furthermore, there is a tube of radius more than
1
about
\Sigma_{i}
. Note if
N(\mathbf{s}_{1})\cong N(\mathbf{s}_{2})
, the number
k
of components of
\Sigma_{1}
must equal the number of components of
\Sigma_{2}
.

Now, consider a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon N(\mathbf{s}_{1})\to N(\mathbf{s}_{2})
. By Mostow rigidity, combined with a theorem of Gabai
[
27
]
,
\varphi
is isotopic to an isometry. (See also Gabai, Meyerhoff, and Thurston
[
28
]
.)
This isometry must carry the shortest
k
-tuple of geodesics in
N(\mathbf{s}_{1})
to the shortest
k
-tuple of geodesics in
N(\mathbf{s}_{2})
. Thus, after adjusting
\varphi
by an isotopy, we may suppose that
\varphi(\Sigma_{1})=\Sigma_{2}
. Hence
\varphi
restricts to a homeomorphism from
N=N(\mathbf{s}_{1})-\Sigma_{1}
to
N=N(\mathbf{s}_{2})-\Sigma_{2}
, sending
\mathbf{s}_{1}
to
\mathbf{s}_{2}
.
∎

When
N
has one cusp, we have the following corollary.

Theorem 7.30
.

Let
N
be a one-cusped hyperbolic
3
–manifold.
Suppose that
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
are distinct slopes on the cusp of
N
, where the normalized length of each
s_{i}
satisfies

L(s_{i})\geq\max\left\{10.1,\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{{\operatorname{sys}}(N)}+58}\right\}.

Then
(s_{1},s_{2})
cannot be a purely cosmetic pair. If
(s_{1},s_{2})
is a chirally cosmetic pair, then there is a symmetry of
N
sending
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
.

In particular,
Conjectures
1.7
and
1.9
both hold for pairs
(s_{1},s_{2})
satisfying the above bound on length.

Proof of Theorem
7.30
.

Suppose there is a homeomorphism
\varphi\colon N(s_{1})\to N(s_{2})
. Then, by Theorem
7.29
,
\varphi
restricts to a homeomorphism of
N
sending
s_{1}
to
s_{2}
. That
\varphi\colon N\to N
must be orientation-reversing follows from a standard argument, as in
[
6
, Lemma 2]
.

Let
\lambda
be the unique null-homologous slope on the cusp of
N
. Thus
\varphi|_{N}
must send
\lambda
to
\lambda
. By Mostow–Prasad rigidity,
\varphi|_{N}
is homotopic to an isometry. If an isometry of
N
is orientation-preserving and stabilizes
\lambda
, then it must stabilize every slope, implying
s_{1}=s_{2}
. Since we have assumed that
s_{1}\neq s_{2}
, it follows that
\varphi|_{N}
is orientation-reversing, hence
\varphi
is also.
∎

7.4.
Controlling multiple geodesics

The following theorem is included here for future use.

Theorem 7.31
.

Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Let
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
and
\Sigma^{+}=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n+k}
be geodesic links in
M
, where
k\geq 1
. Assume that, in the complete structure on
M
, we have
\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)\leq 0.735
. In addition, define

m=\max\,\big\{\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\sigma_{j})\>:\>n+1\leq j\leq n+k\big\}

and assume that
\ell+2m\leq 0.14
.

Then
M_{0}=M-\Sigma
is connected to
M_{4\pi^{2}}=M
via a cone deformation that preserves
\Sigma^{+}
setwise and keeps each component of
\Sigma^{+}
geodesic.

One novelty of
Theorem
7.31
is that it does not care about the total length of
\Sigma^{+}
. All that the theorem needs is for the drilling locus
\Sigma
to be short, and for each separate component of

\Sigma^{+}-\Sigma
to be (uniformly) short. Under these hypotheses, one may use Theorem
7.19
to estimate the change in length of each component of
\Sigma^{+}-\Sigma
.

Proof of Theorem
7.31
.

If
k=1
, that is if
\Sigma^{+}=\Sigma\cup\gamma
for a single closed curve
\gamma
, this theorem is already covered by Theorem
7.19
. In the general case, when
k\geq 2
, the proof closely parallels that proof. Define
R_{0}=h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)\geq\arctanh(1/\sqrt{3})
. By Theorem
5.14
, there is a cone deformation connecting
M-\Sigma
to
M
, which maintains an embedded tube about
\Sigma
of radius at least
R_{0}
.

Next, define

(7.32)

{\hat{R}_{\min}}=h^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+2m+{10^{-5}}))>0.794,

and set
{\hat{Z}_{\min}}=\tanh({\hat{R}_{\min}})
as usual.
Our hypothesis on
\ell+2m
ensures that
2\pi(\ell+2m+{10^{-5}})<{h_{\max}}
, hence Corollary
4.25
ensures that
h^{-1}
is decreasing.
We claim that the above cone deformation can be chosen such that
the following hold for all
t\in[0,4\pi^{2}]
:

(1)

The link
\Sigma^{+}
is a union of geodesics in the cone metric
g_{t}
. Furthermore, the cone deformation preserves
\Sigma^{+}
setwise.

(2)

There is an embedded multi-tube about
\Sigma^{+}
of radius greater than
{\hat{R}_{\min}}
.

(3)

For every curve
\sigma_{j}
with
j>n
, the complex length satisfies

{d_{\mathrm{hyp}}}(\mathcal{L}_{t}(\sigma_{j}),\,\mathcal{L}_{4\pi^{2}}(\sigma_{j}))\>\leq\>(4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell).

Let
I
be the maximal sub-interval of
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
containing
(2\pi)^{2}
, such that conclusions (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) hold for
t\in I
.

First, we show that
(2\pi)^{2}\in I
, hence is non-empty. Note that (
1
) holds by hypothesis, while (
3
) is vacuous for
t=(2\pi)^{2}
. To verify (
2
), choose an arbitrary pair of components
\sigma_{j},\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
with
n<j,j^{\prime}\leq n+k
.
Let
U_{\max}
be the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
in the complete metric on
M
, and let
R
be the smallest radius of a tube in
U_{\max}
.
By Lemma
5.15
, we know
R>0.531
. Now, Corollary
4.25
says that

R\geq h^{-1}(\mathcal{A}(4\pi^{2}))=h^{-1}(2\pi(\ell+2m))>{\hat{R}_{\min}}

where the strict inequality is by definition of
{\hat{R}_{\min}}
. In particular, the tubes of radius
{\hat{R}_{\min}}
about the components of
\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
are pairwise disjoint. Since
j,j^{\prime}
were arbitrary, we conclude that the tubes of radius
{\hat{R}_{\min}}
about
all
components of
\Sigma^{+}
are pairwise disjoint.
Thus (
2
) holds for
t=(2\pi)^{2}
, implying that
I\neq\emptyset
.

To see that
I
is open, let
0<t_{0}\in I
.
By Theorem
5.6
, there is a local cone deformation on
M
that treats
\Sigma^{+}
as its singular locus but does not change the cone angles on
\Sigma^{+}-\Sigma
. Hence (
1
) holds in an open neighborhood of
t_{0}
, as does (
2
) because it is an open condition. Now, Lemma
7.4
implies that (
3
) holds on the union of
I
and this open neighborhood. Hence
I
is open.

To see that
I
is closed, let
a=\inf I
. Since the tube radius about
\Sigma^{+}
must remain
at least

{\hat{R}_{\min}}
by continuity, and in particular does not degenerate, Theorem
5.13
implies that the cone deformation preserving
\Sigma^{+}
setwise can be extended to
t=a
.
Since (
3
) is a closed condition, it holds at
t=a
by continuity. Thus, for every
j>n
and every
t\in[a,(2\pi)^{2}]
,
Lemma
7.14
gives

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{len}_{t}(\sigma_{j})

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\sigma_{j})\cdot\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell))

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi m\,\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell)).

Focusing attention on two components
\sigma_{j}
and
\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
with
j,j^{\prime}>n
, we have

2\pi(\operatorname{len}_{t}(\sigma_{j})+\operatorname{len}_{t}(\sigma_{j^{\prime}}))\leq 4\pi\cdot m\,\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell)).

Meanwhile, by Lemma
7.17
, the visual area of
\Sigma
satisfies

\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma)\>\leq\>\mathcal{A}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}\!\!=\>2\pi\ell\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}

where
Z_{0}=\tanh R_{0}
and the function
q(z)
is given in Lemma
7.17
. Combining the last two equations, we conclude that the total visual area of
\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\leq 2\pi\ell\left(\frac{\sqrt{t}}{2\pi}\right)^{q(Z_{0})}\!\!+4\pi m\,\exp((4\pi^{2}-t)\,F({\hat{Z}_{\min}},\ell))=\hat{f}_{\ell,m}(t).

(Note that
\hat{f}_{\ell,m}(t)
differs from the function
f_{\ell,m}(t)
of Lemma
7.18
in that the second term begins with
4\pi m
instead of
2\pi m
. Note as well that
{\hat{Z}_{\min}}
is defined via (
7.32
), which differs from the definition of
{Z_{\min}}
in Lemma
7.18
.)

By
an interval arithmetic computation in Sage
[
23
]
, exactly as in Lemma
7.18
, we learn that
\hat{f}_{\ell,m}(t)
is bounded above in terms of
\hat{f}_{\ell,m}(4\pi^{2})
.
In symbols, we verify the strict inequality below:

(7.33)

\mathcal{A}_{t}(\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}})\leq\hat{f}_{\ell,m}(t)<2\pi(\ell+2m+{10^{-5}})=h({\hat{R}_{\min}}).

Thus Corollary
4.25
implies that at
t=a
, the maximal tube about
\Sigma\cup\sigma_{j}\cup\sigma_{j^{\prime}}

has smallest radius
R>{\hat{R}_{\min}}
. Since
j,j^{\prime}
were chosen arbitrarily, the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma^{+}
also has smallest radius
R>{\hat{R}_{\min}}
.
This means condition (
2
) holds at
a=\inf I
, hence
I
is closed. Thus
(
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) hold for all
t\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, completing the proof.
∎

8.
Bilipschitz estimates in the thick part

The main result of this section is Theorem
8.3
. The theorem gives an effective bilipschitz bound on the change in geometry during a cone deformation.

Definition 8.1
.

Given Riemannian metrics
g
and
\hat{g}
on a manifold
N
, we define the bilipschitz constant at a point
p\in N
by

(8.2)

{{\rm bilip}}_{p}(g,\hat{g})=\inf\left\{K\geq 1\>\left|\>\frac{1}{K}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\hat{g}(x,x)}{g(x,x)}}\leq K\right.\mbox{for all }x\in T_{p}N-\{0\}\right\}.

The bilipschitz constant between
g
and
\hat{g}
is

{\rm bilip}_{N}(g,\hat{g})=\sup\,\{{{\rm bilip}}_{p}(g,\hat{g}):p\in N\},

with the convention that the supremum of an unbounded set is undefined. In the applications in this paper, the manifold
N
will be compact, hence the supremum will actually be attained.

Theorem 8.3
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.938
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Suppose that one of the following hypotheses holds.

•

In the cusped structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies
L^{2}\geq 107.6/\delta^{2}+14.41
.

•

In the complete structure on
M
, the total length of
\Sigma
is
\ell\leq\delta^{2}/17.11
.

Then there is a cone deformation
M_{t}=(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
interpolating between the complete structures on
M-\Sigma
and
M
. For
0\leq a\leq b\leq(2\pi)^{2}
, the cone deformation defines a natural identity map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(M-\Sigma,g_{a})\to(M-\Sigma,g_{b})
.

Suppose that
W
is a submanifold of
M
such that
W\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t\in(a,b)
. Then the identity map

{\mathop{id}}\colon(W,g_{a})\to(W,g_{b})

is
J
–bilipschitz, where

J=\exp\left(\frac{7.193\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}\right).

The natural identity map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(M,g_{a})\to(M,g_{b})
was defined in Remark
5.18
. It arises because we are keeping the pair of sets
(M,\Sigma)
constant and varying the metric
g_{t}
on
M-\Sigma
according to a canonical choice of
1
–form
\omega
. One important property of this identity map is that it is equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of the pair
(M,\Sigma)
.

We remark that Theorem
8.3
is an effective version of a theorem of Brock and Bromberg
[
11
, Corollary 6.10]
. Our proof follows their outline, with effective control on the boundary terms inserted into the calculation.

The following lemma shows that the hypothesis on
L
of Theorem
8.3
implies the hypothesis on
\ell
. Thus, in proving the theorem, it suffices to assume the hypothesis on
\ell
. In addition, Lemma
8.4
says that we may substitute
\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.41}
in bounding the bilipschitz constant
J
.

Lemma 8.4
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.938
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that in the cusped structure on
M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies
L^{2}\geq 107.6/\delta^{2}+14.41
. Then in the complete structure on
M
, the total length of
\Sigma
is

\ell<\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.41}<\frac{\delta^{2}}{17.11}.

Proof.

Since
\delta\leq 0.938
, we have

L^{2}\geq\frac{107.6}{\delta^{2}}+14.41\geq 136.7=I({Z_{\min}}),

where
{Z_{\min}}\geq 0.9006
. Thus, by Lemma
6.10
, we have

\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-14.41}<\frac{\delta^{2}}{17.11}.\qed

To prove Theorem
8.3
, we recall from Section
5
that the cone deformation is governed by a harmonic form

\omega
. By Equation (
5.2
),
\omega
decomposes as

(8.5)

\omega=\eta+i*D\eta.

It turns out that the real part
\eta=\Re(\omega)
controls the infinitesimal change in metric. Hodgson and Kerckhoff pointed out that the metric inner product
g_{t}(x,y)
between a pair of vectors

x,y\in T_{p}M
satisfies the differential equation

(8.6)

\frac{dg_{t}(x,y)}{dt}=2g_{t}(x,\eta(y)).

See, for instance, the displayed equation on page 46 of
[
32
]
. We remark that (
8.6
) can be used to define the
TM
–valued
1
–form
\eta
, which is how Brock and Bromberg have defined it
[
11
, page 61]
.
Subsequently, they showed that the pointwise norm of
\eta
controls the bilipschitz constant.

Lemma 8.7
.

If
||\eta(p)||\leq K
for all
t\in[a,b]
, then

{{\rm bilip}}_{p}(g_{a},g_{b})\leq e^{(b-a)K}.

Proof.

On
[
11
, pages 61–62]
, Brock and Bromberg show that (
8.6
) implies

\left|\frac{dg_{t}(x,x)}{dt}\right|\leq 2||\eta(p)||\,g_{t}(x,x).

Here, the pointwise norm
||\eta(p)||
should be evaluated at time
t
.
Integrating the above estimate, we obtain

e^{-2(b-a)K}\leq\frac{g_{b}(x,x)}{g_{a}(x,x)}\leq e^{2(b-a)K},\quad\mbox{hence}\quad{{\rm bilip}}_{p}(g_{a},g_{b})\leq e^{(b-a)K}.\qed

8.1.
Bounding the pointwise norm

We will control the pointwise norm
\|\eta(p)\|
by combining the results of Section
6
and the following mean-value inequality due to Hodgson, Kerckhoff, and Bromberg
[
14
]
.

Theorem 8.8
.

Let
\omega
be a harmonic form on a ball
B_{r}(p)\subset{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, where
r<\pi/\sqrt{2}
. Then

(8.9)

\|\omega(p)\|\leq\frac{3\sqrt{2\pi(\sinh(2r)-2r)}}{4\pi f(r)}\sqrt{\int_{B_{r}}\|\omega\|^{2}dV,}

where

f(r)=\cosh(r)\sin(\sqrt{2}r)-\sqrt{2}\sinh(r)\cos(\sqrt{2}r).

Proof.

See Bromberg
[
14
, Theorem 9.9]
. We have substituted the formula
\operatorname{vol}(B_{r}(p))=\pi(\sinh(2r)-2r)
. See, for example, Ratcliffe
[
55
, Exercise 3.4.5]
.
∎

To simplify the bound of (
8.9
), we employ the following estimate.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
0<\delta\leq 0.938
, and let
r=\delta/2
. Then the term in (
8.9
) before the square root of the integral satisfies:

(8.11)

\frac{3\sqrt{2\pi(\sinh(2r)-2r)}}{4\pi f(r)}\leq\left(\frac{1.046}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\right)\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{-3/2}.

If
0<\delta\leq 0.106
and
r=\delta/2
, the bound becomes

(8.12)

\frac{3\sqrt{2\pi(\sinh(2r)-2r)}}{4\pi f(r)}\leq\left(\frac{1.001}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\right)\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{-3/2}.

Proof.

Before giving the proof, we motivate the statement. The function to be bounded in (
8.11
) and (
8.12
) can be expressed as a Puiseux series in the following way:

\frac{3\sqrt{2\pi(\sinh(2r)-2r)}}{4\pi f(r)}=\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\right)r^{-3/2}+\left(\frac{1}{10}\sqrt{\frac{3}{\pi}}\right)r^{1/2}+O(r^{5/2}).

Since
r=\delta/2
, the bounds in (
8.11
) and (
8.12
) pick out the leading term of the series, with a bit of multiplicative error.

Now, we proceed to the proof. Set
C=1.046
.
A bit of algebraic manipulation shows that the desired inequality (
8.11
) is equivalent to

(8.13)

\Phi(r)=2f(r)^{2}C^{2}-3r^{3}(\sinh(2r)-2r)\geq 0\qquad\text{on}\qquad r\in[0,0.469].

Since
f(r)=\cosh(r)\sin(\sqrt{2}r)-\sqrt{2}\sinh(r)\cos(\sqrt{2}r)
is an analytic function, the entire function
\Phi(r)
in (
8.13
) is analytic.
The
9
-th degree Taylor polynomial for
\Phi(r)
, centered at
r=0
, is

P(r)=4(C^{2}-1)r^{6}-\tfrac{4}{5}(C^{2}+1)r^{8}.

By Taylor’s theorem with remainder, when
r\in[0,0.469]
we have

\Phi(r)-P(r)=\frac{\Phi^{(10)}(\rho)}{10!}r^{10},\qquad\text{for some }\rho\in[0,0.469]\text{ depending on }r.

Using interval arithmetic in Sage
[
23
]
, we verify that
\Phi^{(10)}(\rho)/10!\geq-0.085
for all
\rho\in[0,0.469]
. Thus

\Phi(r)\geq 4(C^{2}-1)r^{6}-\tfrac{4}{5}(C^{2}+1)r^{8}-0.085r^{10},

a function that is easily seen to be non-negative for
r\in[0,0.469]
because it factors into linear and quadratic terms.
This proves (
8.13
) and therefore (
8.11
).

Inequality (
8.12
) is proved similarly, substituting
C=1.001
.
∎

Proposition 8.14
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.938
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
with total length
\ell\leq\delta^{2}/17.11
. Let
M_{t}
be a cone-manifold occurring along a deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
. Let
p\in M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
.
Then

\|\omega(p)\|\leq\frac{0.1822\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}.

Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that
t
lies in the interior of the deformation interval
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, or in other words, we have cone angle
0<\alpha<2\pi
along every component of
\Sigma
. Once we establish the desired bound on
\|\omega(p)\|
for such times in the interior, the general case will follow by continuity.

For
p\in M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
, by Definition
2.14
there is a round ball of radius
\delta/2
centered at
p
, which is isometric to a ball in
{\mathbb{H}}^{3}
. For each component
\sigma_{j}
of
\Sigma
, let
r_{j}(\delta)
be the tube radius of the
\delta
–thin tube about
\sigma_{j}
in the metric
g_{t}
. By Definition
6.15
, this means that every point
q\in\partial U_{r_{j}(\delta)}
satisfies

{\operatorname{injrad}}(q)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}(q,\,U_{r_{j}(\delta)})=\delta/2,

where the inequality is Lemma
2.19
.
Set
{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}=(r_{1}(\delta)-\delta/2,\ldots,r_{n}(\delta)-\delta/2)
.
By Theorem
6.20
, the multi-tube
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}
is embedded.
Moreover,
U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}\cap B_{\delta/2}(p)=\emptyset
. Therefore, by (
6.3
) and Theorem
6.20

(8.15)

\int_{B_{\delta/2}(p)}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV\leq\int_{M-U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV=b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\left(\frac{\ell}{7.935\,\delta}\right)^{2}.

The bound on the term in (
8.9
) before the square root of the integral is bounded by Lemma
8.10
, particularly equation (
8.11
).
Now, we plug the estimates of (
8.15
) and (
8.11
) into (
8.9
) to obtain

\|\omega(p)\|\leq\left(\frac{1.046}{\delta^{3/2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{6}{\pi}}\right)\left(\frac{\ell}{7.935\,\delta}\right)\leq\frac{0.1822\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}.\qed

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
8.3
.

Proof of Theorem
8.3
.

By Lemma
8.4
, the hypothesis on
L
implies the hypothesis on
\ell
, so we may assume the hypothesis on
\ell
. The existence of a cone deformation
(M,\Sigma,g_{t})
now follows from Theorem
5.1
.
Recall as well that in Remark
5.18
, we made a canonical choice of harmonic form
\omega
governing the family of cone-metrics
g_{t}
, and used this choice to define a natural identity map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(M-\Sigma,g_{a})\to(M-\Sigma,g_{b})
.

Now, we can check the bilipschitz estimate of the theorem.
Set
K=0.1822\,\ell\,\delta^{-5/2}
. Suppose that
W\in M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t\in(a,b)
and that
p\in W
. Then, by (
8.5
) and Proposition
8.14
, we have

\|\eta(p)\|\leq\|\omega(p)\|\leq K.

Set

J=\exp\left((2\pi)^{2}K\right)=\exp\left((2\pi)^{2}\cdot\frac{0.1822\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{7.193\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}\right).

By Lemma
8.7
, the bound
\|\eta(p)\|\leq K
implies

{\rm bilip}_{p}(g_{a},g_{b})\leq e^{|b-a|K}\leq e^{(2\pi)^{2}K}=J,

as claimed.
∎

8.2.
Corollaries and variations

Theorem
8.3
has the following pair of corollaries on effective bilipschitz bounds on drilling and filling.
In both statements,
g_{0}
denotes the complete hyperbolic metric on
M-\Sigma
, and
g_{4\pi^{2}}
denotes the complete hyperbolic metric on
M
.

Corollary 8.16
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.938
and
J>1
.
Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold. Let
\Sigma\subset M
be a geodesic link whose total length
\ell

satisfies

\ell\leq\min\left\{\frac{\delta^{2}}{17.11},\,\frac{\delta^{5/2}\log(J)}{7.193}\right\}.

Let
W\subset M
be any submanifold such that
W\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t
. Then, for all
a,b\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, the identity map

{\mathop{id}}\colon(W,g_{a})\to(W,g_{b})
is
J
–bilipschitz.

Corollary 8.17
.

Fix any
0<\delta\leq 0.938
and any
J>1
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that in the cusped structure on
N=M-\Sigma
, the total normalized length
L
of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies

L^{2}\geq\max\left\{\frac{107.6}{\delta^{2}}+14.41,\,\frac{45.20}{\delta^{5/2}\log(J)}+14.41\right\}.

Let
W\subset M
be any submanifold such that
W\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t
. Then, for all
a,b\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, the identity map

{\mathop{id}}\colon(W,g_{a})\to(W,g_{b})
is
J
–bilipschitz.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
8.3
and Lemma
8.4
.
∎

We also have the following analogue of Theorem
8.3
, with stronger hypotheses and a stronger bilipschitz estimate. This stronger statement will be used in Section
9
.

Theorem 8.18
.

Fix
0<\delta\leq 0.106
and
1<J\leq e^{1/5}
.
Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Suppose that in the complete structure on
M
, the total length of
\Sigma
is bounded as follows:

\ell\leq\frac{\delta^{5/2}\log(J)}{3.324}\quad\text{if}\quad\delta\leq 0.012,\qquad\quad\ell\leq\frac{\delta^{5/2}\log(J)}{3.498}\quad\text{if}\quad 0.012<\delta\leq 0.106.

Let
[a,b]\subset[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
be an interval of time, and suppose that
W
is a submanifold of
M
such that
W\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t\in(a,b)
. Then the identity map

{\mathop{id}}\colon(W,g_{a})\to(W,g_{b})

is
J
–bilipschitz.

Proof.

First, suppose that
\delta\leq 0.012
. Since
\log(J)\leq 1/5
and
5\times 3.324=16.62
, our hypotheses are stronger than those of Proposition
6.27
.
The proof that
{\mathop{id}}
is
J
–bilipschitz is almost identical to the proof of Theorem
8.3
, with two small substitutions.
Inside the proof of Proposition
8.14
, we replace (
8.15
), which uses Theorem
6.20
, by the estimate of Proposition
6.27
:

(8.19)

\int_{B_{\delta/2}(p)}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV\leq\int_{M-U_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}}\|\omega\|^{2}\,dV=b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)\leq\left(\frac{\ell}{16.432\,\delta}\right)^{2}.

We also replace (
8.11
) by (
8.12
).
Now, using (
8.12
) and (
8.19
) in place of (
8.11
) and (
8.15
), Proposition
8.14
becomes

(8.20)

\|\omega(p)\|\leq\left(\frac{1.001}{\delta^{3/2}}\,\sqrt{\frac{6}{\pi}}\right)\left(\frac{\ell}{16.432\,\delta}\right)\leq\frac{0.08419\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}.

Plugging (
8.20
) into the final part of the proof of Theorem
8.3
gives

{\rm bilip}(g_{a},g_{b})\leq\exp\left((2\pi)^{2}\cdot\frac{0.08419\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{3.324\,\ell}{\delta^{5/2}}\right)\leq J.

If
0.012<\delta\leq 0.106
, the proof is again almost identical; we use Proposition
6.26
instead of Proposition
6.27
to get a slightly looser bound on
b_{{\mathbf{r}}_{-}}(\eta,\eta)
.
∎

9.
Margulis numbers for cone-manifolds

Our goal in this section is to give an effective estimate on the Margulis numbers of cone-manifolds that occur in the cone-deformations we have been studying. See Theorems
9.26
and
9.27
for effective statements in this vein. These estimates for cone-manifolds are used to control the Margulis number of the non-singular manifold
M
at the end of the deformation, under hypotheses on either the drilled manifold
M-\Sigma
(in Theorem
9.25
), or the filled manifold
M
(in Theorem
9.29
).

The proof of each of these results breaks into a topological statement and a geometric statement. The topological statement is Theorem
9.1
, which can be paraphrased as follows: so long as

\epsilon
is a Margulis number for an initial manifold
M_{0}
, and so long as the
\epsilon
–thick part
M_{0}^{\geq\epsilon}
stays
\delta
–thick in
M_{t}
for every
t
, we learn that
\delta
is a Margulis number for
M_{t}
. The geometric statement is Theorem
9.15
: under strong hypotheses on length, the
\epsilon
–thick part
M_{0}^{\geq\epsilon}
indeed stays almost
\epsilon
–thick in
M_{t}
for all
t
.
In fact, both the topological and the geometric statement require geometric hypotheses about
\ell
or
L
, and rely on the estimates in the preceding sections.

Theorem
9.15
has an additional application: it allows us to formulate a version of the bilipschitz Theorem
8.3
whose hypotheses are only on the non-singular manifold at one end of a cone-deformation, without any pre-existing knowledge about intermediate cone manifolds. See Theorem
9.30
for details.

9.1.
Tubes realizing injectivity radii

The following theorem says that Margulis numbers in a cone-manifold
M_{t}
are related to Margulis numbers in
M_{0}
, provided that we have set containment of the corresponding thin parts.

Theorem 9.1
.

Fix

0<\delta\leq\epsilon
, where
\delta<0.9623
. Suppose
M
is a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold,
and
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
, while the total length of
\Sigma
satisfies

(9.2)

\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\leq\min\left\{0.261\delta,\>\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)\right\}.

Let
M_{0}
be the complete metric on
M-\Sigma
, and assume that
\epsilon
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
. Suppose as well that for every
t
, we have

(9.3)

(M_{t}^{\leq\delta}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}.

Then
\delta
is a Margulis number for
M_{t}
, for each
t
.

The hypotheses on
\delta
,
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})
, and
\ell
in Theorem
9.1
match those of Lemma
6.16
.
Recall that the function in (
9.2
), which expresses the upper bound on
\ell
in terms of
\delta
, is depicted in Figure
6.1
on page
6.1
.

The proof of Theorem
9.1
breaks up into several steps.
In Proposition
9.4
, we show that
if
M_{t}
is a cone-manifold occurring in the interior of our deformation, and
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)\leq\delta/2
, then an appropriate subset of
M^{\leq\delta}
is
(loosely speaking) realized by a tube through
x
. This “tube”
U
may be immersed rather than embedded, may be singular, and may be a horocusp. There may also be more than one such tube through
x
. In Lemma
9.9
, we will show that, under the hypotheses of Theorem
9.1
, these tubes or cusps are in fact disjointly embedded in a singular cone-manifold
M_{t}
. This proves Theorem
9.1
for singular manifolds
M_{t}
, corresponding to a time parameter
t<(2\pi)^{2}
.

We complete the proof of Theorem
9.1
via a continuity argument. The function
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)
is not always continuous in
t
, but it comes close; the precise (and more subtle) continuity statement is established in Lemma
9.10
.

Recall from Definition
2.8
that a non-trivial group element
\varphi\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
is called peripheral if a loop representing
\varphi
is freely homotopic into a cusp of
M-\Sigma
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Fix

0<\delta<0.9623
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold,
and
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
, while
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)
satisfies (
9.2
).

Let
M_{t}
be a cone-manifold in the interior of the deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
.
Let
x\in M_{t}
be a point such that
2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)\leq\delta
. Choose a lift
\widetilde{x}\in\hat{M}_{t}
, the universal branched cover, let
\varphi\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
be a group element guaranteed by Lemma
2.11
, so that
d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})=2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)
, and let
G=C(\varphi)
be the centralizer of
\varphi
in
\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma).

Then the following hold.

(1)

G
stabilizes an open set
\widetilde{V}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
, which is either a horoball or a regular neighborhood of a geodesic.

(2)

The quotient
V=\widetilde{V}/G
admits a local isometry
f\colon V\to M_{t}
. Thus
f
is an immersed tube or immersed horocusp in
M_{t}
, as in Definition
2.16
.

(3)

We have
V^{\geq 1.51}\neq\emptyset
.

(4)

There is a sub-tube or sub-horocusp
U\subset V
and a point
y\in\partial U
,
such that
x=f(y)
. Furthermore,
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)
.

(5)

If
\varphi
is peripheral, then
f|_{\overline{U}}\colon\overline{U}\to M_{t}
is an embedding
of a horocusp or singular tube.

Proof.

Let
U_{\max}(\Sigma)=U_{1}\cup\ldots\cup U_{n}
be the maximal multi-tube about
\Sigma
, as in Definition
4.2
. Let
U_{n+1},\ldots,U_{m}
be horoball neighborhoods of the cusps of
M_{t}
(if any), expanded until each
U_{j}
bumps into a previously expanded cusp or tube. By Lemma
6.16
,
each tube
U_{j}
has radius
R_{j}
, where

R_{j}\geq{R_{\min}}=h^{-1}(2\pi\ell)\geq 0.7555.

By Theorem
4.26
, every point
z\in\partial U_{j}
satisfies

2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(z,U_{j})>1.1227\tanh{R_{\min}}-0.1604.

Furthermore, by Remark
4.35
, this bound applies to both tubes and horocusps. Combining this with (
6.18
) in Lemma
6.16
, we learn that

(9.5)

2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(z,U_{j})>1.1227\tanh{R_{\min}}-0.1604\geq\delta

for every point
z\in\partial U_{j}
on the boundary of a tube or horocusp.

To begin proving the conclusions of the proposition, suppose first that
\varphi
is peripheral. Then Lemma
2.9
says that
\varphi
stabilizes either a horoball in
\hat{M}_{t}
or a singular geodesic
\hat{\sigma}_{j}\subset\hat{\Sigma}
, where
\hat{\Sigma}
is the preimage of
\Sigma
. In both cases, we will see that
x\in U_{j}
for a tube or horocusp
U_{j}
constructed above.

If
\varphi
stabilizes a singular geodesic
\hat{\sigma}_{j}\subset\hat{\Sigma}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
, then it stabilizes the universal branched cover
\widetilde{U}_{j}
of some singular tube
U_{j}
. Alternately,
\varphi
stabilizes the universal cover
\widetilde{U}_{j}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
of some horocusp
U_{j}
. In either case, we claim
that
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{U}_{j}
. This is because
\varphi
moves
\widetilde{x}
by distance
2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)\leq\delta
, whereas (
9.5
) implies that
\varphi
moves every point outside
\widetilde{U}_{j}
by distance strictly greater than
\delta
. Thus
\widetilde{x}\in\widetilde{U}_{j}
, hence
x\in U_{j}
.

We can now construct the sets
U
and
V
claimed in the proposition. Let
\widetilde{V}
be the maximal metric neighborhood of
\widetilde{U}_{j}
that is disjoint from
\hat{\Sigma}
, except possibly at the core of
\widetilde{U}_{j}
. Let
\widetilde{U}\subset\widetilde{U}_{j}
be the proper sub-tube or sub-horoball defined by the property that
\widetilde{x}\in\partial\widetilde{U}
.
Set
G=C(\varphi)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
and consider the covering projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{V}\to\widetilde{V}/G
.
Then we have a sequence of local isometries

(9.6)

V=\widetilde{V}/G\hookrightarrow\hat{M}_{t}/G\longrightarrow M_{t},

whose composition we call
f
. Restricting attention to
U_{j}=\pi(\widetilde{U}_{j})
, we recover the embedding
f\colon U_{j}\hookrightarrow M_{t}
. Since
\overline{U}\subset U_{j}
, it follows that
f|_{\overline{U}}
is an embedding as well. Since
\widetilde{x}\in\partial\widetilde{U}
, we have a point
y=\pi(\widetilde{x})\in\partial U
such that
f(y)=x
. Furthermore, since
\varphi\in G
, we have

2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)=d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})=2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U).

This proves all the properties claimed of
U
and
V
, except for (
3
). We will check (
3
) after verifying the corresponding property in the non-peripheral case in Claim
9.7
.

Next, suppose that
\varphi
is non-peripheral. Then Lemma
2.9
says that
\varphi
stabilizes
a geodesic axis
\widetilde{\beta}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
, not contained in the singular locus, which covers a closed geodesic
\beta\subset M_{t}
.
Observe that
\beta
cannot be entirely contained in a singular tube
U_{j}
, because the only closed geodesic in
U_{j}
is the singular core
\sigma_{j}
.

Furthermore, for every
U_{j}
, we have
d(\sigma_{j},\partial U_{j})\geq{R_{\min}}\geq 0.7555.
Thus if
\beta\cap U_{j}
reaches the singular core
\sigma_{j}
, then
\operatorname{len}(\beta\cap U_{j})\geq 2\cdot 0.7555=1.511
. However,

\operatorname{len}(\beta\cap U_{j})\leq\operatorname{len}(\beta)\leq d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})\leq\delta<0.9623.

So any geodesic arc
\beta\cap U_{j}
cannot reach the singular core
\sigma_{j}
, implying that
\beta\cap\Sigma=\emptyset
. Consequently,
\widetilde{\beta}
is disjoint from the singular locus
\hat{\Sigma}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
.

Let
\widetilde{V}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
be the maximal tubular neighborhood of
\widetilde{\beta}
that is disjoint from the singular locus
\hat{\Sigma}
. This neighborhood has finite radius because
\hat{M}_{t}
contains singular points. Then
\widetilde{V}
is stabilized by the maximal cyclic subgroup
G=C(\varphi)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\widetilde{\beta}/G=\beta
. Note that
\varphi\in G
.
Define a model tube
V=\widetilde{V}/G
. Then, as in (
9.6
), we have a sequence of local isometries

V=\widetilde{V}/G\hookrightarrow\hat{M}_{t}/G\longrightarrow M_{t},

whose composition we call
f
.
Now, we have to show that the immersion
f\colon V\to M_{t}
has the properties claimed in the proposition.

Claim 9.7
.

We have
V^{\geq 1.51}\neq\emptyset
.

Recall that
\widetilde{V}
was defined to be the maximal open tube about
\widetilde{\beta}
that is disjoint from
\hat{\Sigma}
. Thus there is a point
\widetilde{z}\in\partial\widetilde{V}\cap\hat{\Sigma}
. Let
\widetilde{z}^{\prime}
be a closest translate of
\widetilde{z}
by a non-trivial element of
G
. Then
\widetilde{z},\widetilde{z}^{\prime}
must lie on distinct singular geodesics in
\hat{\Sigma}
that cover the same component
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
. Since the tube
U_{j}
about
\sigma_{j}
must have radius
R_{j}\geq 0.7555
, it follows that

d(\widetilde{z},\widetilde{z}^{\prime})\geq 2R_{j}\geq 2{R_{\min}}>1.51.

Compare Claim
4.8
for a very similar setup.

Let
\pi\colon\hat{M}_{t}\to\hat{M}_{t}/G
be the covering projection, and let
z=\pi(\widetilde{z})=\pi(\widetilde{z}^{\prime})\in\partial V
.
Since
\widetilde{z},\widetilde{z}^{\prime}
are a pair of closest lifts of
z
under
G
, we have

{\operatorname{injrad}}(z,V)>1.51.

In particular,
V^{\geq 1.51}\neq\emptyset
. (The same argument applies to the tube or horocusp
V
in the peripheral case, completing the proof of the proposition in that case.)

In the non-peripheral case, we have now checked that the immersion
f\colon V\to M_{t}
satisfies properties (
1
)–(
3
). It remains to show that there is a sub-tube
U\subset V
satisfying (
4
). Note that (
5
) is vacuous for non-peripheral elements.

Claim 9.8
.

There exists a tube
U
with
\overline{U}\subset V
and a point
y\in\partial U
, such that
x=f(y)
and
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)
.

Recall that
\widetilde{x}\in\hat{M}_{t}
and
\varphi\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
were chosen to have the property that

d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})=2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)\leq\delta
. In particular,
\varphi\widetilde{x}
is a closest translate of
\widetilde{x}
in
\hat{M}_{t}
.
Recall as well that
\varphi\in G
. Letting
y=\pi(\widetilde{x})=\pi(\varphi\widetilde{x})\in\hat{M}_{t}/G
, we get

2{\operatorname{injrad}}(y)=d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})=2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)\leq\delta<1,

where
{\operatorname{injrad}}(y)
denotes the injectivity radius of
y
in the cone-manifold
\hat{M}_{t}/G
.

On the other hand, by Claim
9.7
,

V\subset\hat{M}_{t}/G

extends out to include points of injectivity radius
1.51
in
\hat{M}_{t}/G
. Thus
y\in V
.

Let
U\subset V
be the model tube such that
y\in\partial U
. Then

{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,V)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U),

because the realizing isometry
\varphi
belongs to
G=\pi_{1}V=\pi_{1}U
.

By construction, the local isometry
f\colon V\to M_{t}
is a restriction of the covering map
\hat{M}_{t}/G\to M_{t}
. Thus
f(y)=f\circ\pi(\widetilde{x})=x
, completing the proof of (
4
).
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Fix

0<\delta\leq\epsilon
, where
\delta<0.9623
. Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold, and let
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
, while the total length of
\Sigma
satisfies (
9.2
).

Let
M_{0}
be the complete metric on
M-\Sigma
, and assume that
\epsilon
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
. For a cone-manifold
M_{t}
in the interior of the cone-deformation from
M_{0}
to
M_{4\pi^{2}}
, suppose that

(M_{t}^{\leq\delta}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}.

Then
\delta
is a Margulis number for
M_{t}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in M_{t}
be a point such that
2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)=\delta
. Choose a lift
\widetilde{x}\in\hat{M}_{t}
. By Lemma
2.11
, there is a group element
\varphi\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
such that
d(\widetilde{x},\varphi\widetilde{x})=\delta
. By Proposition
9.4
,
\varphi
defines a closed model tube or horocusp
\overline{U}
with an isometric immersion
f\colon\overline{U}\to M_{t}
, such that
x\in f(\partial U)
.

Note that the isometry
\varphi
may not be unique. The proof amounts to checking that
\overline{U}
is unique and that
f
is an embedding.

If
\varphi
is peripheral, Proposition
9.4
tells us that
U
is a singular tube or horocusp and that
f
is an embedding.
Furthermore,
there exists
y\in\partial\overline{U}
such that
x=f(y)
and

{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)
. Since local isometries can only reduce the injectivity radius (by Lemma
2.19
) and since
y\in\partial\overline{U}
, every point
z\in\overline{U}
satisfies

{\operatorname{injrad}}(f(z))\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}(z,U)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)={\operatorname{injrad}}(x)=\delta/2.

Then
f(\overline{U})\subset M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
, hence by hypothesis
f(\overline{U})-\Sigma\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
.
Let
W
be the component of
M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
containing
f(\overline{U})-\Sigma
. Since
U
is a horocusp or singular tube, it follows that
W
must be a horocusp of
M_{0}
. Here, we are using the hypothesis that
\epsilon
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
.

Suppose that
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
also has the property that
d(\widetilde{x},\varphi^{\prime}\widetilde{x})=\delta
. Let
U^{\prime}
be the tube or horocusp associated to
\varphi^{\prime}
, with an isometric immersion
f^{\prime}\colon U^{\prime}\to M_{t}
and with
x\in f^{\prime}(\partial U^{\prime})
.
Since
x\in\overline{U^{\prime}}\cap W
, the hypotheses of the lemma imply
(f(\overline{U^{\prime}})-\Sigma)\subset W
. In particular,
\varphi^{\prime}\in f^{\prime}_{*}\pi_{1}(U^{\prime})\subset\pi_{1}(W)
, where
\pi_{1}(W)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}
is a peripheral subgroup of
\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
.
It follows that
U
and
U^{\prime}
are either both horocusps or both tubes about the same component of
\Sigma
, hence
U^{\prime}
is also embedded. Since
x\in\partial U
and
x\in\partial U^{\prime}
, it follows that
\overline{U}=\overline{U^{\prime}}
is the full component of
M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
containing
x
.

One particular consequence of the above paragraph is that if
\varphi
is peripheral, then
\varphi^{\prime}
must also be peripheral.

If
\varphi
is non-peripheral, Proposition
9.4
tells us that there is a non-singular immersed tube
f\colon U\to M_{t}
and a point
y\in\partial U
such that
x=f(y)
and
{\operatorname{injrad}}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)
.
Since isometric immersions can only reduce injectivity radius, every point
z\in\overline{U}
satisfies

{\operatorname{injrad}}(f(z))\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}(z,U)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}(y,U)={\operatorname{injrad}}(x)=\delta/2.

Thus
f(\overline{U})\subset(M_{t}^{\leq\delta}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
.
Let
W
be the component of
M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
containing
f(\overline{U})
. Since the core of
f(U)
is a non-singular geodesic
\beta\subset M_{t}
,
it follows that
W
must be a non-singular, embedded tube in
M_{0}
. Recall that
\epsilon
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
.

We check that
f
is an embedding by considering the universal branched cover
\hat{M}_{t}
. Recall from the construction of Proposition
9.4
, specifically from (
9.6
), that the universal cover
\widetilde{U}
is identified with a tubular neighborhood of a geodesic axis
\widetilde{\beta}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
, and that
\pi_{1}(U)=G=C(\varphi)
, the stabilizer of
\widetilde{\beta}
. Let
\widetilde{W}\subset\hat{M}_{t}
be a component of the preimage of
W
such that
\widetilde{U}\subset\widetilde{W}
. Viewed in the complete hyperbolic metric of
\widetilde{M-\Sigma}=\widetilde{M_{0}}={\mathbb{H}}^{3}
, the component
\widetilde{W}
is a tubular neighborhood of a geodesic, whose translates by elements of
\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
are either disjoint from
\widetilde{W}
or coincide with
\widetilde{W}
. Thus a translate
\eta\widetilde{U}
for
\eta\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
is either disjoint from
\widetilde{W}
or contained in
\widetilde{W}
. In the first case, we have
\eta\widetilde{U}\cap\widetilde{U}=\emptyset
, and in fact
\eta(\partial\widetilde{U})\cap\partial\widetilde{U}=\emptyset
. In the second case,
\eta
must stabilize the endpoints of
\widetilde{\beta}
, in which case
\eta\in G
stabilizes
\widetilde{\beta}
and also
\widetilde{U}
. Thus
\widetilde{U}\cup\partial\widetilde{U}
is precisely invariant under the action of
\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
, which means the quotient tube
\overline{U}
embeds in
M_{t}
.

Finally, suppose that
\varphi^{\prime}\in\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
also has the property that
d(\widetilde{x},\varphi^{\prime}\widetilde{x})=\delta
. Let
U^{\prime}
be the tube associated to
\varphi^{\prime}
, with an isometric immersion
f^{\prime}\colon U^{\prime}\to M_{t}
. We already checked that the peripheral and non-peripheral cases cannot overlap, so
U^{\prime}
must also be a non-singular tube. By the same argument as above,
f^{\prime}
must be an embedding, hence we consider
\overline{U}
and
\overline{U^{\prime}}
to be subsets of
M_{t}
. As above, all of
\overline{U^{\prime}}
must be
\delta
–thin in
M_{t}
, hence
\overline{U^{\prime}}\subset W\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
. Since
x\in\overline{U}\cap\overline{U^{\prime}}\subset W
, both
\pi_{1}(U)
and
\pi_{1}(U^{\prime})
are subgroups of
\pi_{1}(W)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
. Furthermore, both
\pi_{1}(U)
and
\pi_{1}(U^{\prime})
are generated by primitive elements of
\pi_{1}(M-\Sigma)
, hence the two generators of
{\mathbb{Z}}
must coincide up to inverses, hence the cores of
U
and
U^{\prime}
map to the same geodesic
\beta\subset M_{t}
. Since
x\in\partial U
and
x\in\partial U^{\prime}
, it follows that
\overline{U}=\overline{U^{\prime}}
is the full component of
M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
containing
x
.
∎

Lemma
9.9
establishes the conclusion of Theorem
9.1
for all
t<(2\pi)^{2}
, when the cone-manifold
M_{t}
is actually
singular
. To finish the proof of Theorem
9.1
, we need a continuity argument as
t\to(2\pi)^{2}
. This is somewhat subtle, as injectivity radius can be discontinuous as a function of
t
precisely when
t\to(2\pi)^{2}
.

We fix the following notation throughout.
Let
M_{t}
be a cone-manifold occurring along a deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
, with metric
g_{t}
. Let
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)
denote the injectivity radius of
x
in the
g_{t}
metric, and let
d_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
denote distance in the
g_{t}
metric.

Lemma 9.10
.

Fix

0<\delta<0.9623
. Suppose
M
is a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold,
and
\Sigma=\sigma_{1}\cup\ldots\cup\sigma_{n}
is a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose that
\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.0996
for every
j
, while
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)
satisfies (
9.2
).

Consider the cone-deformation
M_{t}
from
M-\Sigma
to
M
.
Then, for every
x\in M
and every
b<(2\pi)^{2}
,
we have

\lim_{t\to b}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x).

Furthermore, for
b=(2\pi)^{2}
,

\left(\!d_{b}(x,\Sigma)\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\quad\text{or}\quad\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)\geq\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\quad\text{implies}\quad\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)={\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x).

Proof.

By Definition
2.10
,
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)
varies continuously under a continuous change in cone metric when the cone-manifold stays singular, that is,
t<(2\pi)^{2}
. This proves the first assertion of the lemma.

As
t\to b=(2\pi)^{2}
and
\alpha\to 2\pi
, a discontinuity can arise in the following restricted way. If the cone angle on
\Sigma
is
\alpha<2\pi
, a non-singular ball about
x
cannot have radius larger than
d_{t}(x,\Sigma)
, hence
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)\leq d_{t}(x,\Sigma)
. For
\alpha\geq\pi
, and for points sufficiently close to
\Sigma
,
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)
can in fact be equal to
d_{t}(x,\Sigma)
. As
t\to b
and
\alpha\to 2\pi
, the link
\Sigma
becomes non-singular, allowing
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x)
to suddenly become larger than
d_{b}(x,\Sigma)
at time
b=(2\pi)^{2}
. Thus the “furthermore” statement also holds automatically, unless
x
is a point satisfying

d_{b}(x,\Sigma)=\lim_{t\to b^{-}}d_{t}(x,\Sigma)=\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x).

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
x\in M
is a point that satisfies

(9.11)

d_{b}(x,\Sigma)=\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)\geq\delta/2.

Then the distance
h=d_{b}(x,\Sigma)
is realized by a geodesic segment
\beta
(in the non-singular
g_{b}
metric) from
x
to some component
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
. Let
y\in\beta
be the point such that
d_{b}(y,\sigma_{j})=\delta/2
, hence
d_{b}(x,y)=h-\delta/2
. We will obtain a contradiction by estimating
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y)
for
t
near
b
, in two different ways. First, Lemma
3.1
implies
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y)\geq{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)-d_{t}(x,y)
for every
t
. Taking limits, we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)-\lim_{t\to b^{-}}d_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=h-(h-\delta/2)

(9.12)

\displaystyle=\delta/2.

On the other hand, since
d_{t}(y,\sigma_{j})
is a continuous function of
t
, it must be the case that for all
t<b
sufficiently close to
b
, we have
d_{t}(y,\sigma_{j})\leq 1.0006(\delta/2)
. For all such
t
, Lemma
6.16
says that there is an embedded
\delta
–thin tube
U_{t}=U_{t}(\sigma_{j})
about
\sigma_{j}
, of radius
r_{j}(\delta)\geq 1.001(\delta/2)
in the
g_{t}
metric. Thus
y\in U_{t}
, and furthermore,

d_{t}(y,\partial U_{t})\geq 0.0004(\delta/2)=0.0002\delta.

By Lemma
6.16
,
U_{t}
is a
\delta
–thin tube, meaning
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(z,U_{t})=\delta/2
for every
z\in\partial U_{t}
. Define
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
by the property that

0.0002\delta=\arccosh\sqrt{\frac{\cosh\delta-1}{\cosh\delta^{\prime}-1}}.

Then the upper bound of Theorem
3.6
implies that
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y,U_{t})\leq\delta^{\prime}/2
. Combining this with Lemma
2.19
, we obtain

(9.13)

{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(y,U_{t})\leq\delta^{\prime}/2\qquad\text{for all $t<b$ sufficiently close to $b$.}

But now (
9.13
) contradicts (
9.12
), because
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
. Thus no point
x\in M
can satisfy
(
9.11
), completing the proof.
∎

We can now complete the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Given Lemma
9.9
, it remains to prove that
\delta
is a Margulis number for
M_{4\pi^{2}}
.

Set
b=4\pi^{2}
. For every
t<b
, and every component
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
, Lemma
6.16
gives an embedded
\delta
–thin tube
U_{t}=U_{t}(\sigma_{j})
, whose radius (in the
g_{t}
metric) is at least
(1.001)\delta/2
.
By Proposition
9.4
, every point

x_{t}\in\partial U_{t}
satisfies
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x_{t})={\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x_{t},U_{t})=\delta/2
.
By Lemma
6.16
, the tubes about different components of
\Sigma
are disjointly embedded.
As
t\to b
, each tube
U_{t}(\sigma_{j})
converges (in the Hausdorff metric) to a tube
U_{b}(\sigma_{j})
. Every convergent sequence of points
\{x_{t}\in\partial U_{t}\}
limits to a point
x_{b}\in\partial U_{b}
, with
d_{b}(x_{b},\sigma_{j})=d_{b}(x_{b},\Sigma)>\delta/2
. Thus, by Lemma
9.10
,

{\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x_{b})=\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x_{b})=\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x_{t})=\delta/2.

We conclude that for every
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
, there is a tube
U_{b}(\sigma_{j})\subset M_{b}^{\leq\delta}
, such that
\partial U_{b}(\sigma_{j})
consists of points where
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}
is continuous in
t
. By Lemma
9.10
, all points outside these tubes also have the property that
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}
is continuous in
t
. Thus we may apply continuity arguments outside the multi-tube
U_{b}(\Sigma)
.

Recall that for every
t
, we have
(M_{t}^{\leq\delta}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}.

Since
M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
is open and
\bigcup_{t}M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
is closed, by continuity, there is a value
\delta_{+}>\delta
such that

(M_{t}^{\leq\delta_{+}}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}.

Thus Lemma
9.9
implies that
\delta_{+}
is a Margulis number for
M_{t}
for all
t<b
. For all
t<b
sufficiently close to
b
, we have
M_{b}^{\leq\delta}\subset M_{t}^{\leq\delta_{+}}
. By choosing
\delta_{+}
sufficiently close to
\delta
and
t
sufficiently close to
b
, we can ensure that every component of
M_{t}^{\leq\delta_{+}}
contains a component of
M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
.

With this setup, let
U_{b}
be an arbitrary component of
M_{b}^{\leq\delta}
. We will see that
U_{b}
is a tube or horocusp by showing that

U_{b}=\lim_{t\to b}U_{t}
, where
U_{t}\subset M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
is a
\delta
–thin tube or cusp in the
g_{t}
metric.

For
t
close to
b
, our chosen component
U_{b}
is contained in a tube or horocusp component of
M_{t}^{\leq\delta^{+}}
, which contains a
\delta
–thin tube or horocusp
U_{t}\subset M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
. As
t\to b
, these tubes or horocusps
U_{t}
converge in the Hausdorff topology to
U_{b}
. Note that disjoint components
U_{t},U_{t}^{\prime}\subset M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
cannot collide as
t\to b
because they are contained in disjoint components of
M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
. Similarly, a component
U_{t}
cannot collide into itself because distinct lifts of
U_{t}
to
\hat{M}_{b}
are contained in disjoint preimages of a component of
M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
. Thus every
U_{b}
is an embedded tube or horocusp.
∎

9.2.
Thick parts stay almost as thick

Next, we show that under strong hypotheses on the length
\ell
, the thick part of a cone-manifold
M_{a}
stays almost as thick in every other
M_{t}
. This will enable us to apply Theorem
9.1
and control Margulis numbers. We begin with the following strengthened version of Theorem
3.6
, which applies to an entire cone-manifold instead of a tube.

Proposition 9.14
.

Fix
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
, and
0<\delta\leq\epsilon^{2}/7.256
. Suppose
M
is a complete, finite volume hyperbolic manifold,
and
\Sigma
is a geodesic link in
M
.
Suppose
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\leq 0.261\delta
.
Then, for all
t
,

d(M_{t}^{\leq\delta},M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon})\geq\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{7.256\delta}}\right)-0.1475.

Furthermore, if
\epsilon\leq 0.3
, then

d(M_{t}^{\leq\delta},M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon})\geq\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{7.256\delta}}\right)-0.0424.

Proof.

We will use Proposition
9.4
. To check the hypotheses of that proposition, observe that our hypotheses require
0<\delta\leq(\log 3)^{2}/7.256<0.1664
. For
\delta
in this range, Equation (
9.2
) becomes the simpler statement
\ell\leq 0.261\delta
, which is what we require here. Similarly, every component
\sigma_{j}\subset\Sigma
must have

\operatorname{len}(\sigma_{j})\leq 0.261\cdot 0.1664<0.0996
. Thus Proposition
9.4
applies under our hypotheses.

Now, let
x\in M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
. By Proposition
9.4
, there is an immersed tube (or immersed cusp)
f\colon V\to M_{t}
, such that
x=f(y)
for some point
y\in V
, and
