{\operatorname{injrad}}(y,V)={\operatorname{injrad}}(x).

Furthermore,
V^{\geq 1.51}\neq\emptyset
, hence
V^{\geq\epsilon}\neq\emptyset
. By Theorem
3.6
, we have

d(V^{\leq\delta},V^{\geq\epsilon})\geq\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{7.256\delta}}\right)-0.1475=:h.

Consequently, every point
y^{\prime}\in V
such that
d(y,y^{\prime})<h
must lie in
V^{<\epsilon}
.

It follows that
f(V^{<\epsilon})
contains the
h
–neighborhood of
x=f(y)
. Thus every point
x^{\prime}\in M_{t}
such that
d(x,x^{\prime})<h
must be the image of some
y^{\prime}\in V^{<\epsilon}
, hence Lemma
2.19
gives

2{\operatorname{injrad}}(x^{\prime})\leq 2{\operatorname{injrad}}(y^{\prime},V)<\epsilon.

We conclude that every point of
M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon}
lies further than
h
from
x\in M_{t}^{\leq\delta}
.

Finally, if
\epsilon\leq 0.3
, we can use Theorem
3.7
instead of Theorem
3.6
, and repeat the same argument with
h=\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{7.256\delta}}-0.0424
.
∎

Theorem 9.15
.

Fix
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
and
1<J\leq e^{1/5}
.
Let
M
be a complete, finite volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma\subset M
a geodesic link. Suppose that
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)
is bounded as follows:

(9.16)

\ell\leq\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{471.5\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.0424)}\quad\text{if}\quad 0<\epsilon\leq 0.3,

or

(9.17)

\ell\leq\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{496.1\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}\quad\text{if}\quad 0.3\leq\epsilon\leq\log 3.

Then, for every
a,t\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, the manifolds
M_{a}
and
M_{t}
in the deformation from
M-\Sigma
to
M
satisfy

(9.18)

M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}\subset M_{t}^{>\epsilon/J}.

Moreover, let
B
be a closed ball of radius
\epsilon/2
in the
g_{a}
metric about a point
p\in M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}
. Then

{\rm bilip}_{B}(g_{a},g_{t})<J
.

\begin{overpic}[JHypoth.png]
\end{overpic}

Figure 9.1.
The function of
J
that provides an upper bound on
\ell
in Equation (
9.16
), for the value
\epsilon=0.292
. For any fixed
\epsilon
, this function has a global maximum when
J\leq e^{1/5}
.

Theorem
9.15
is reminiscent of a result of Brock and Bromberg
[
11
, Theorem 6.11]
. Furthermore, the crawling argument employed in the proof below is inspired by the one used in
[
11
, Theorem 6.11]
. However, the statement of Theorem
9.15
is stronger than that of
[
11
, Theorem 6.11]
in two distinct ways. First, following the theme of this paper, Theorem
9.15
is effective. Second, Theorem
9.15
provides better control over quantifiers. Brock and Bromberg’s theorem says that for all sufficiently small
\epsilon
,
there exists

\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
such that
M_{t}^{\leq\epsilon^{\prime}}\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
. Meanwhile, Theorem
9.15
says that for all
\epsilon\leq 0.3
, and
for all

\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon/J<\epsilon
, we have
M_{t}^{\leq\epsilon^{\prime}}\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
, provided
\ell
is sufficiently short.

The seemingly artificial hypothesis
J\leq\exp(1/5)
in Theorem
9.15
is justified as follows. For fixed
\epsilon
, the function in (
9.16
) or (
9.17
) is
not
monotonic in
J
: it starts out at
0
when
J=1
, rises to a maximum, and then decreases toward
0
. Note that the function factors as
(\log(J)/J^{5})
times a term that is decreasing in
J
. Hence the maximum of the function, corresponding to the mildest hypotheses on
\ell
, always occurs when
\log J\leq 1/5
.
(See Figure
9.1
, and compare Lemma
9.24
.) For
J>\exp(1/5)
, the theorem requires stronger hypotheses on
\ell
but produces a weaker conclusion, hence it makes sense to exclude those values.

Proof of Theorem
9.15
.

For most of the proof, suppose that
\epsilon\leq 0.3
. This means we are working under the hypothesis (
9.16
).

Fix an arbitrary
a\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
. For the length of the proof, we will treat
a
as a constant and
t
as a variable. Set

(9.19)

\delta=\frac{(\epsilon/J)^{2}}{7.256\cosh^{2}(J\epsilon/2+0.0424)}\,,\quad\text{so that}\quad\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon/J}{\sqrt{7.256\,\delta}}\right)-0.0424=J\epsilon/2.

It follows that
\delta\leq 0.012
. For this value of
\delta
, we will actually prove the closely related condition

(9.20)

d_{a}(M_{t}^{\leq\delta},\,M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon})>\epsilon/2.

As we shall see at the end of the proof, equation (
9.20
) quickly implies (
9.18
).

Let
I
be the maximal sub-interval of
[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, containing
a
, such that (
9.20
) holds for all
t\in I
. First, we check that
I
is non-empty. This follows from Proposition
9.14
:

d_{a}(M_{a}^{\leq\delta},M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon})\geq\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon/J}{\sqrt{7.256\,\delta}}\right)-0.0424=J\epsilon/2>\epsilon/2.

Thus
a\in I
, hence
I
is nonempty. Also,
I
is open because (
9.20
) involves a strict inequality, hence is an open condition.
We will show
I
is closed, which will imply that
I=[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
.

Consider what can be said about
\overline{I}
. Let
b=\sup I
. Lemma
9.10
tells us that if
x\in M
satisfies
\lim_{t\to b^{-}}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{t}(x)\geq\delta/2
, then
{\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x)\geq\delta/2
as well. A stronger form of continuity holds for
\inf I
. In other words, Lemma
9.10
and the definition of
I
imply

(9.21)

d_{a}(M_{t}^{\leq\delta},\,M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon})\geq\epsilon/2\quad\mbox{for all $t\in\overline{I}$.}

Figure 9.2.
The proof of Theorem
9.15
. The left and right panels illustrate how the region near
q
looks in the
g_{a}
metric and
g_{b}
metric, respectively. Black objects are in the
g_{a}
metric and blue objects are in the
g_{b}
metric.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
I
is not closed, hence either
\sup I\notin I
or
\inf I\notin I
. We start by handling the supremum
b=\sup I
.
Suppose, in contradiction to (
9.20
), that there exist points
p\in\partial M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}
and

q\in\partial M_{b}^{\leq\delta}
such that
d_{a}(p,q)=\epsilon/2
.
Since
p\in M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}
, there is an embedded ball
B
centered at
p
, of radius
\epsilon/2
in the
g_{a}
metric, such that

q\in\partial B
. See Figure
9.2
.

We will apply Theorem
8.18
to
B
. To check the hypotheses, note that
(
9.19
) and
\epsilon\leq 0.3
imply
\delta\leq 0.012
. In addition,
note that

3.324\,(7.256)^{5/2}=471.415\ldots<471.5,

hence
(
9.16
) and (
9.19
) imply

(9.22)

\ell\leq\frac{(\epsilon/J)^{5}\log J}{471.5\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.0424)}=\frac{\delta^{5/2}\log J^{\prime}}{3.324}<\frac{\delta^{5/2}\log J}{3.324}.

where
J^{\prime}
is ever so slightly less than
J
.
Finally, (
9.21
) implies
B\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t\in\overline{I}
.

Thus we may apply Theorem
8.18
and get a
J^{\prime}
–bilipschitz diffeomorphism on
B
.
One direction of Theorem
8.18
says that distances from
p
to points of
\partial B
can only shrink by a factor of
J^{\prime}<J
as we change metrics from
g_{a}
to
g_{b}
. Thus
B
contains a ball
B^{\prime}
of radius
\epsilon/(2J^{\prime})
in the
g_{b}
metric, implying that
p\in M_{b}^{\geq\epsilon/J}
. On the other hand, since
d_{a}(p,q)=\epsilon/2
, the bilipschitz upper bound of Theorem
8.3
implies

(9.23)

d_{b}(p,q)\leq J^{\prime}\epsilon/2<J\epsilon/2.

Now, Proposition
9.14
and (
9.19
) imply that the distance between thick and thin parts in the
g_{b}
metric satisfies

d_{b}(M_{b}^{\leq\delta},M_{b}^{\geq\epsilon/J})\geq\arccosh\left(\frac{\epsilon/J}{\sqrt{7.256\,\delta}}\right)-0.0424=J\epsilon/2.

But then
d_{b}(p,q)\geq J\epsilon/2
, which contradicts (
9.23
).
This contradiction implies that
b=\sup I\in I
. By the same argument,
\inf I\in I
. Thus
I
is closed, hence (
9.20
) stays true for all
t\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
.

Now, we can conclude the proof of the theorem for
0<\epsilon\leq 0.3
.
For any
p\in M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}
, there is an embedded ball
B
centered at
p
, of radius
\epsilon/2
in the
g_{a}
metric. By (
9.20
), we have
B\subset M_{t}^{\geq\delta}
for all
t
, hence Theorem
8.18
applies to give a
J^{\prime}
–bilipschitz diffeomorphism on
B
. Thus, as in the above argument, we learn that for every
t
,
B
contains a ball
B^{\prime}
, centered at
p
, of radius
\epsilon/(2J)
in the
g_{t}
metric. Therefore,
p\in M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon/J}
, and (
9.18
) holds.

Finally, if
0.3<\epsilon\leq\log 3
, the argument is identical apart from slightly different numbers. We define

\delta=\frac{(\epsilon/J)^{2}}{7.256\cosh^{2}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}<0.106,

which means that hypothesis (
9.17
) enables us to apply the version of Theorem
8.18
for
\delta\leq 0.106
. Then we employ a crawling argument to prove (
9.20
) for this value of
\delta
, which implies (
9.18
).
∎

9.3.
Applications

Combining Theorems
9.1
and
9.15
gives several results about the behavior of Margulis numbers under filling and drilling. We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 9.24
.

For
0\leq\epsilon\leq\log 3
and
1\leq J\leq e^{1/5}
, consider the function

g(\epsilon,J)=\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{496.1\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}

occurring in (
9.17
). The maximum value of
g
on this domain is
5.609\ldots\times 10^{-5}
, achieved when
\epsilon=\log 3
and
J=1.15203\ldots
. Furthermore,
g(\epsilon,J)
is increasing
\epsilon
on its entire domain and increasing in
J
when
J\in[1,1.15]
.

We remark that
g(\epsilon,J)
is
not
increasing in
J
on its entire domain; compare Figure
9.1
.

Proof.

To see that
g(\epsilon,J)
is increasing in
\epsilon
, we compute the partial derivative:

\frac{\partial g}{\partial\epsilon}=\frac{5\epsilon^{4}\log J}{496.1\,J^{5}\cosh^{6}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}\cdot\big[\cosh\big(\tfrac{J\epsilon}{2}+0.1475\big)-\tfrac{J\epsilon}{2}\sinh\big(\tfrac{J\epsilon}{2}+0.1475\big)\big].

The first term in the above product is non-negative on the whole domain, and positive whenever
\epsilon>0
and
J>1
. To analyze the second term, we substitute
x=J\epsilon/2
and verify that the function
(\cosh(x+0.1475)-x\sinh(x+0.1475))
is positive whenever
x\in[0,1]
. Since
J\epsilon/2<1
on the entire domain, it follows that
\partial g/\partial\epsilon\geq 0
on the entire domain.

In a similar fashion, we compute
\partial g/\partial J
:

\frac{\partial g}{\partial J}=\frac{\tfrac{5}{2}\epsilon^{5}}{496.1\,J^{6}\cosh^{6}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}\cdot\big[\left(\tfrac{2}{5}-2\log J\right)\cosh\big(\tfrac{J\epsilon}{2}+0.1475\big)-\epsilon J\log J\sinh\big(\tfrac{J\epsilon}{2}+0.1475\big)\big]

The first term in the product is non-negative on the whole domain, and positive when
\epsilon>0
. Using Sage
[
23
]
, we verify that the second term is positive when
J\leq 1.15
, hence
g(\epsilon,J)
is increasing in
J
on this sub-domain.

Finally, we check the assertion about the maximum value of
g(\epsilon,J)
. By monotonicity in
\epsilon
, any maximum occurs when
\epsilon=\log 3
. We verify using Sage that
g(\log 3,J)<5.610\times 10^{-5}
for every
J
in the domain. We also check directly that the function attains a value greater than
5.609\times 10^{-5}
at
J=1.15203
. See the ancillary files
[
23
]
for full details.
∎

Theorem 9.25
.

Fix
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
and
1<J\leq e^{1/5}
. Let
N
be a cusped hyperbolic
3
–manifold such that
\epsilon
is a Margulis number of
N
. Let
\mathbf{s}
be a tuple of slopes on cusps of
N
whose normalized length
L=L(\mathbf{s})
satisfies

\frac{2\pi}{L(\mathbf{s})^{2}-11.7}\leq\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{496.1\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}=g(\epsilon,J).

Then
\delta=\min\{\epsilon/J,\,0.962\}
is a Margulis number for
M=N(\mathbf{s})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.24
,
g(\epsilon,J)<5.61\times 10^{-5}
for all
\epsilon,J
.
Consequently, any tuple of slopes
\mathbf{s}
satisfying the hypotheses of the theorem must have normalized length
L=L(\mathbf{s})\geq 334
.
By Theorem
5.17
,
there is a cone deformation from
M_{0}=N
to
M_{4\pi^{2}}=M=N(\mathbf{s})
maintaining a tube of radius
{R_{\min}}
about the singular locus
\Sigma
, where
{Z_{\min}}=\tanh{R_{\min}}\geq 0.9998
. Consequently, Lemma
6.10
says that the length of
\Sigma
in the complete metric on
M
is

\ell=\operatorname{len}_{4\pi^{2}}(\Sigma)\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-11.7}\leq g(\epsilon,J).

By Lemma
9.24
,
g(\epsilon,J)
is increasing in
J
when
J\leq 1.15
. Thus, given
\delta=\min\{\epsilon/J,\,0.962\}
as in the theorem statement, we define
J^{\prime}=\epsilon/\delta=\max\{J,\epsilon/0.962\}
and obtain

\ell\leq g(\epsilon,J)\leq g(\epsilon,J^{\prime}).

By Theorem
9.15
, every cone-manifold
M_{t}
occurring in the deformation satisfies
M_{0}^{\geq\epsilon}\subset M_{t}^{>\epsilon/J^{\prime}}=M_{t}^{>\delta}
. Taking complements of thick parts, and removing
\Sigma
, we obtain
(M_{t}^{\leq\delta}-\Sigma)\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
.

We conclude the proof using Theorem
9.1
. The one hypothesis of that theorem that remains to be checked is equation (
9.2
). The first inequality of (
9.2
) holds because

\ell\leq g(\epsilon,J^{\prime})=\frac{(\epsilon/J^{\prime})^{5}\,\log J^{\prime}}{496.1\cosh^{5}(J^{\prime}\epsilon/2+0.1475)}<\frac{\delta^{5}}{496.1}\ll 0.261\delta.

For the second inequality of (
9.2
), we only need to consider
\delta\in[\delta_{\rm cut},0.962]
, where
\delta_{\rm cut}
is as in the proof of Lemma
6.16
; compare Figure
6.1
.
By Lemma
4.22
and Remark
4.23
, the function
{\operatorname{haze}}(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227})
is decreasing in this range. Thus

\ell\leq g(\epsilon,J^{\prime})<5.61\times 10^{-5}<1.44\times 10^{-4}<\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{0.962+0.1604}{1.1227}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}{\operatorname{haze}}\left(\frac{\delta+0.1604}{1.1227}\right),

hence (
9.2
) holds for every pair
(\epsilon,J)
. Thus we may use Theorem
9.1
to conclude that
\delta
is a Margulis number for every
M_{t}
, and in particular for the non-singular metric on
M
.
∎

Theorem
9.25
is useful in a situation where we have information about
N=M-\Sigma
and its (optimal) Margulis number. However, Theorems
9.1
and
9.15
can also be used in a situation where we have information about
M
and its short geodesics.

Theorem 9.26
.

Let
M
be a (non-singular) finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold with
k=0
,
1
, or
2
cusps.
Suppose the
(3-k)
shortest geodesics in
M
have total length at most
5.56\times 10^{-5}
. Let
\Sigma
denote the union of these geodesics. Then the geodesics are disjointly embedded, there exists a cone-deformation
M_{t}
interpolating between the complete structure on
M-\Sigma
and the complete structure on
M
, and for all
t
, the optimal Margulis number for
M_{t}
is greater than
0.9536
.

Proof.

If
M
contains
(3-k)
closed geodesics of total length at most
5.56\times 10^{-5}
, then by Meyerhoff’s theorem
[
44
]
, those geodesics are disjointly embedded. By Theorem
5.1
, there is a cone-deformation
M_{t}
interpolating between the complete structure on
M-\Sigma
and the complete structure on
M
. Observe that
M_{0}=M-\Sigma
has three cusps. Thus
b_{1}(M_{0})\geq 3
, hence Theorem
1.5
.(
3
) says that
\epsilon=\log 3
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
. Setting
J=1.152
gives

\ell\leq 5.56\times 10^{-5}<\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{496.1\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.1475)}\,.

(As in Lemma
9.24
, the value
J=1.152
was chosen because it very nearly places the mildest possible hypotheses on
\ell
.) Now, by Theorem
9.15
, we have
M_{t}^{\leq\epsilon/J}\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
for every
t
. Since
\ell
is small enough to satisfy equation (
9.2
) for
\delta=\epsilon/J
, Theorem
9.1
implies that
\epsilon/J>0.9536
is a Margulis number for every
M_{t}
.
∎

Theorem 9.27
.

Let
M
be a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold. Let
\ell={\operatorname{sys}}(M)
denote the length of a shortest geodesic
\Sigma\subset M
, and assume
\ell\leq 0.0996
. Then there exists a cone-deformation
M_{t}
interpolating between the complete structure on
M_{0}=M-\Sigma
and
M=M_{4\pi^{2}}
. Furthermore, the following hold for every
M_{t}
.

(1)

If
\ell\leq 2.93\times 10^{-7}
, then for any
t\in[0,4\pi^{2}]
,
the optimal Margulis number for
M_{t}
is greater than
0.2408
.

(2)

If
\ell\leq 2.73\times 10^{-8}
, then for any
t\in[0,4\pi^{2}]
, the optimal Margulis number for
M_{t}
is greater than
0.29
.

Proof.

Since
\ell\leq 0.0996
, Theorem
5.1
implies the cone-deformation
M_{t}
exists.

For (
1
), assume
\ell\leq 2.93\times 10^{-7}
.
By Theorem
1.5
.(
4
),
\epsilon=0.292
is a Margulis number for
M_{0}
. Now, set
J=1.2124
. Plugging in these values of
\epsilon
and
J
yields

(9.28)

\ell\leq 2.93\times 10^{-7}<\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J}{471.5\,J^{5}\cosh^{5}(J\epsilon/2+0.0424)}\,.

(As above, the value
J=1.2124<e^{1/5}
is chosen because it very nearly maximizes the function in (
9.28
), placing the mildest possible hypotheses on
\ell
.
See Figure
9.1
.)
Now, by Theorem
9.15
, we have
M_{t}^{\leq\epsilon/J}\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
for every
t
.
Since
\ell
is small enough to satisfy equation (
9.2
) for
\delta=\epsilon/J
, Theorem
9.1
implies
\epsilon/J>0.2408
is a Margulis number for every
M_{t}
.

Item (
2
) is obtained by an identical argument. Suppose that
\ell\leq 2.73\times 10^{-8}
, and set
\epsilon=0.292
and
J=1.00689<0.292/0.29
.
By Theorem
9.15
, we have
M_{t}^{\leq\epsilon/J}\subset M_{0}^{<\epsilon}
for every
t
.
Thus, by Theorem
9.1
, we have that
\epsilon/J>0.29
is a Margulis number for every
M_{t}
in this case.
∎

The above results imply the following.

Theorem 9.29
.

Let
M
be a non-singular hyperbolic 3-manifold.

(1)

If
\mu(M)\leq 0.2408
, then
M
is closed and
\operatorname{vol}(M)\leq 36.12
. Furthermore,
{\operatorname{sys}}(M)\geq 2.93\times 10^{-7}
.

(2)

If
\mu(M)\leq 0.29
, then
M
is closed and
\operatorname{vol}(M)\leq 52.78
. Furthermore,
{\operatorname{sys}}(M)\geq 2.73\times 10^{-8}
.

(3)

If
\mu(M)\leq 0.9536
, then
M
has finite volume and
k\in\{0,1,2\}
cusps. The
(3-k)
shortest geodesics in
M
have total length at least
5.56\times 10^{-5}
.

Proof.

We begin by proving (
1
).
Let
M
be a hyperbolic 3-manifold with
\mu(M)\leq 0.2408
. By Theorem
1.5
.(
4
),
M
must be closed. By a theorem of Shalen
[
57
, Theorem 7.1]
, we have
\operatorname{vol}(M)\leq 36.12
. Let
\Sigma
be the shortest closed geodesic in
M
. Then Theorem
9.27
.(
1
) implies
\ell=\operatorname{len}(\Sigma)\geq 2.93\times 10^{-7}
.

Turning to (
2
), let
M
be a hyperbolic 3-manifold with
\mu(M)\leq 0.29
. Then again, a theorem of Shalen
[
57
, Theorem 7.1]
implies that
\operatorname{vol}(M)\leq 52.78
, and Theorem
9.27
.(
2
) implies
{\operatorname{sys}}(M)\geq 2.73\times 10^{-8}
.

To check (
3
), suppose that
\mu(M)\leq 0.9536
. Then, by Theorem
1.5
.(
3
), we know
\operatorname{vol}(M)<\infty
. Since a
k
–cusped manifold has
b_{1}(M)\geq k
, the same theorem implies that
M
has
0\leq k\leq 2
cusps.
Then Theorem
9.26
implies that the
(3-k)
shortest geodesics in
M
have total length at least
5.56\times 10^{-5}
.
∎

As a final application of the results of this section, we have a version of the bilipschitz theorem Theorem
8.3
whose hypotheses are only on a non-singular manifold
M
, rather than the a cone-manifolds
M_{t}
occurring in the middle of the deformation.

Theorem 9.30
.

Fix
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
. Let
M
be a finite-volume hyperbolic 3-manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
. Let
N=M-\Sigma
. Suppose that one of the following hypotheses holds:

(1)

In the complete structure on
M
, the total length of
\Sigma
satisfies

(9.31)

\ell\leq\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6771\cosh^{5}(0.6\epsilon+0.1475)}.

(2)

In the complete structure on
N=M-\Sigma
, the total length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies

(9.32)

L^{2}\geq\frac{2\pi\cdot 6771\cosh^{5}(0.6\epsilon+0.1475)}{\epsilon^{5}}+11.7.

Then there is a cone-deformation
M_{t}
connecting the complete hyperbolic metric
g_{0}
on
N
to the complete hyperbolic metric
g_{4\pi^{2}}
on
N
. Furthermore, the cone deformation gives a natural identity map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(M-\Sigma,g_{0})\to(M-\Sigma,g_{4\pi^{2}})
, such that
{\mathop{id}}
and
{\mathop{id}}^{-1}
restrict to

{\mathop{id}}|_{N^{\geq\epsilon}}\colon N^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow M^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},\qquad{\mathop{id}}^{-1}|_{M^{\geq\epsilon}}\colon M^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow N^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},

which are
J
–bilipschitz inclusions for

J=\exp\left(\frac{11.35\,\ell}{\epsilon^{5/2}}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-11.7}.

Furthermore, for any
x\in M^{\geq\epsilon}\cup N^{\geq\epsilon}
, we have
\frac{1}{1.2}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{0}(x)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}_{4\pi^{2}}(x)\leq 1.2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}_{0}(x)
.

We remark that the strong hypotheses on
\ell
or
L
are driven by Theorem
9.15
. Under these strong hypotheses, we do get very tight control on the bilipschitz constant:
for every
\epsilon\leq\log 3
, the theorem gives a value
J\in(1,\,1.0005)
.

Proof of Theorem
9.30
.

First, we check that hypothesis (
2
) implies hypothesis (
1
).
By Lemma
9.24
(substituting the value
J_{0}=1.2
), the right-hand side of (
9.31
) is increasing in
\epsilon
, hence the right-hand side of (
9.32
) is decreasing in
\epsilon
. Thus the loosest possible upper bound on
L^{2}
occurs when
\epsilon=\log 3
, and implies
L^{2}\geq 116,321
. As in the proof of Theorem
9.25
, we can now conclude using Lemma
6.10
that

\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-11.7},

hence the hypothesis on
L
implies the one on
\ell
, as claimed. By Theorem
5.14
and Lemma
6.10
, the estimate
\ell\leq\frac{2\pi}{L^{2}-11.7}
also holds under hypothesis (
1
).

Now, set
J_{0}=1.2
. Then our hypotheses imply

\ell\leq\frac{\epsilon^{5}\log J_{0}}{496.1J_{0}^{5}\cosh^{5}(J_{0}\epsilon/2+0.1475)}.

Hence Theorem
9.15
applies. By Theorem
9.15
, we have

M^{\geq\epsilon}\subset M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},\qquad N^{\geq\epsilon}\subset M_{t}^{\geq\epsilon/1.2}\qquad\mbox{ for all }t.

The last conclusion of Theorem
9.15
also implies that for any
a,b\in[0,(2\pi)^{2}]
, a point
x\in M_{a}^{\geq\epsilon}
satisfies
\frac{1}{1.2}{\operatorname{injrad}}_{a}(x)\leq{\operatorname{injrad}}_{b}(x)\leq 1.2\,{\operatorname{injrad}}_{a}(x)
. In particular, this holds when
\{a,b\}=\{0,(2\pi)^{2}\}
.

To complete the proof, we set
W=N^{\geq\epsilon}
and apply Theorem
8.3
with
\delta=\epsilon/1.2
. By Theorem
8.3
, the cone-deformation provides a natural
J
–bilipschitz map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(W,g_{0})\to(W,g_{4\pi^{2}})
, as desired. Applying Theorem
8.3
to
W=M^{\geq\epsilon}
gives the reverse
J
–bilipschitz inclusion
{\mathop{id}}^{-1}
.
∎

Corollary 9.33

(Theorem
1.2
)
.

Fix any
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
and any
J>1
. Let
M
be a finite-volume hyperbolic
3
–manifold and
\Sigma
a geodesic link in
M
whose total length
\ell
satisfies

\ell\leq\min\left\{\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6771\cosh^{5}(0.6\epsilon+0.1475)},\,\frac{\epsilon^{5/2}\log(J)}{11.35}\right\}.

Then, setting
N=M-\Sigma
,
there are natural
J
–bilipschitz inclusions

\varphi\colon M^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow N^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},\qquad\psi\colon N^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow M^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},

which are equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of the pair
(M,\Sigma)
.

Proof.

The
J
–bilipschitz inclusions
\varphi
and
\psi
are restrictions of the natural identity maps
{\mathop{id}}
and
{\mathop{id}}^{-1}
from Theorem
9.30
. Because
{\mathop{id}}
is defined by a canonical harmonic form
\omega
, in Remark
5.18
, it is equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of
(M,\Sigma)
.
∎

Corollary 9.34
.

Fix any
0<\epsilon\leq\log 3
and any
J>1
. Let
M
be a
3
–manifold with empty or toroidal boundary, and
\Sigma
a link in
M
.
Suppose that
N=M-\Sigma
admits a complete, finite volume hyperbolic metric where the total normalized length of the meridians of
\Sigma
satisfies

L^{2}\geq\max\left\{\frac{2\pi\cdot 6771\cosh^{5}(0.6\epsilon+0.1475)}{\epsilon^{5}}+11.7,\>\frac{2\pi\cdot 11.35}{\epsilon^{5/2}\log(J)}+11.7\right\}.

Then
M
admits a complete hyperbolic metric in which
\Sigma
is isotopic to a union of geodesics. Furthermore, there are natural
J
–bilipschitz inclusions

\varphi\colon M^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow N^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},\qquad\psi\colon N^{\geq\epsilon}\hookrightarrow M^{\geq\epsilon/1.2},

which are equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of the pair
(M,\Sigma)
.

Proof.

By Theorem
5.17
,
M
is hyperbolic and
\Sigma
is a union of geodesics. Now, the
J
–bilipschitz inclusion
\varphi
is a restrictions of the natural identity map
{\mathop{id}}\colon(M-\Sigma,g_{0})\to(M-\Sigma,g_{4\pi^{2}})
from Theorem
9.30
, while
\psi
is a restriction of
{\mathop{id}}^{-1}
. By Remark
5.18
, the identity map
{\mathop{id}}
is equivariant with respect to the symmetry group of
(M,\Sigma)
, hence so are
\varphi
and
\psi
.
∎

Appendix A
Hyperbolic trigonometry

This appendix records several elementary facts that are used throughout the paper.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
z=\tanh r
and
x=e^{r}
. Then

e^{r}=\sqrt{\frac{1+z}{1-z}},\qquad\sinh r=\frac{xz}{1+z}=\frac{z}{\sqrt{1-z^{2}}},\qquad\cosh r=\frac{x}{1+z}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-z^{2}}}.

Proof.

We may solve the (quadratic) equation

z=\frac{x-x^{-1}}{x+x^{-1}}\quad\mbox{to find}\quad x=\sqrt{\frac{1+z}{1-z}}.

Now, substituting the formula for
x
into

\sinh r=x\cdot\frac{1-x^{-2}}{2},\qquad\cosh r=x\cdot\frac{1+x^{-2}}{2}

gives the remaining identities.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
0<r<s
. Then

\frac{\cosh s}{\cosh r}<e^{s-r}<\frac{\sinh s}{\sinh r}.

Proof.

Let
h=s-r
. Then

\displaystyle\cosh(s)

\displaystyle=\cosh(r+h)

\displaystyle=\cosh r\cosh h+\sinh r\sinh h

\displaystyle<\cosh r\cosh h+\cosh r\sinh h

\displaystyle=\cosh r\cdot e^{h},

proving the first inequality. The second inequality is proved similarly.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose that
0<s\leq{s_{\max}}
and
\tanh{s_{\max}}\leq{z_{\min}}\leq\tanh r
.
Then

\sinh(r-s)\geq\sinh r\cdot f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}})\geq e^{r}\frac{{z_{\min}}}{1+{z_{\min}}}\cdot f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}}),

where

f(s,z)=\cosh s-z^{-1}\sinh s.

Proof.

We set
z=\tanh r
and compute:

\displaystyle\sinh(r-s)

\displaystyle=\sinh r\cosh s-\cosh r\sinh s

\displaystyle=\sinh r\cosh s-z^{-1}\sinh r\sinh s

\displaystyle=\sinh r\cdot f(s,z).

Since
s>0
and
z=\tanh r\in(0,1)
, it follows that

\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}>0\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\partial f}{\partial s}<0.

Therefore,

f(s,z)\geq f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}}),

proving the first inequality of the lemma. For the second inequality, note that the hypothesis
\tanh{s_{\max}}\leq{z_{\min}}
implies
f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}})\geq 0
.
Now, we obtain

\sinh r\cdot f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}})=e^{r}\frac{z}{1+z}\cdot f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}})\geq e^{r}\frac{{z_{\min}}}{1+{z_{\min}}}\cdot f({s_{\max}},{z_{\min}}),

where the equality is Lemma
A.1
and the inequality is the monotonicity of
\frac{z}{1+z}
.
∎
